ANSWER_SHORT<<
Java features a strictly typed, class-based object-oriented model where all reference types descend from java.lang.Object. Java enforces single class inheritance (extends) to prevent the diamond problem, but allows multiple interface implementation (implements); all non-static, non-final, non-private methods are virtual by default; and memory is managed automatically by the JVM Garbage Collector.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Java's OOP model prioritizes architectural safety and enterprise maintainability:
1. Strict Single Class Inheritance: A class can extend only one superclass, avoiding the diamond problem entirely.
2. Interfaces: Pure contracts that allow multiple inheritance of type. Since Java 8, interfaces can contain default and static methods; Java 9 added private interface helper methods.
3. Virtual by Default: Unlike C++, methods in Java are polymorphic by default unless explicitly sealed with the 'final' keyword.
4. Access Levels: public, protected (package + subclasses), private, and package-private (no modifier; visible only within the same package).
5. The 'final' Keyword: Applied to variables (constant reference), methods (prevents overriding), and classes (prevents inheritance, e.g., java.lang.String).
6. JVM Garbage Collection: Objects are allocated on the managed heap and reclaimed via generational tracing garbage collectors (G1, ZGC).
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Deep Technical Breakdown of Java OOP Architecture:

1. Object Root and Virtual Method Table:
Every class implicitly inherits from java.lang.Object, acquiring methods: equals(), hashCode(), toString(), getClass(), clone(), and wait()/notify(). In the HotSpot JVM, each object header contains a Mark Word (hash code, GC age, lock state) and a Klass Word (pointer to instance metadata and vtable).

2. Preventing Multiple Inheritance Ambiguities:
Class multiple inheritance was deliberately excluded by James Gosling to avoid the C++ diamond problem and pointer adjustment complexities. Multiple interface implementation is safe because interfaces traditionally had no state.
When default method collisions occur (e.g. Class C implements A and B, both offering default foo()):
- Class method wins over interface defaults.
- If both are interface defaults, the compiler forces C to override foo() explicitly and disambiguate via A.super.foo().

3. Package-Private Access (Default):
Java provides a 4th access level: omitting any access modifier restricts visibility to the defining package. This facilitates clean module packaging, allowing public entry points while keeping internal collaborators hidden from external packages.

4. Sealed Classes (Java 17+):
Permits fine-grained inheritance control: public sealed class Shape permits Circle, Square {}. No other class can extend Shape, enabling exhaustive pattern matching.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine Java is like school rules! You can only have one biological parent class (no confusing family trees allowed), but you can earn lots of club badges (Interfaces)! Every toy you build is made out of special safe plastic that a friendly robot janitor (Garbage Collector) recycles when you are done playing.
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
In Java, every single piece of code must live inside a class. When you make an object, it is placed into a managed memory area called the Heap. You never have to write 'delete' because Java watches your objects and cleans them up automatically.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
Think of the 'final' keyword in Java. If you label a variable 'final', its value cannot change. If you label a method 'final', derived classes cannot override it. If you label a class 'final' (like String), nobody can inherit from it. It's like putting a permanent lock on your code.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Java classes support single inheritance and multiple interface implementation. Methods are virtual by default. Abstract classes can have state and constructors; interfaces define contracts. The JVM handles memory via tracing garbage collection.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Overriding vs Overloading in Java: Overriding requires identical method signature and compatible (covariant) return type, marked with @Override. Java resolves overloading statically at compile time and overriding dynamically at runtime via vtables.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
HotSpot JVM JIT Inlining and Devirtualization: If the JVM detects at runtime that a virtual method has only one loaded implementation (monomorphic call site), it optimizes the indirect call into a direct inline instruction, eliminating virtual dispatch overhead completely.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Memory Footprint: On 64-bit JVMs with Compressed OOPs enabled (-XX:+UseCompressedOops), an empty Object consumes 16 bytes (12-byte header + 4-byte alignment padding). This header overhead makes arrays of small objects significantly more memory-intensive than equivalent C++ flat structs.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
Banking Core Account Architecture: An abstract Account class provides base balance logic and a unique account ID. Bank policies use interfaces like InterestBearing, Auditable, and TaxDeductible. SavingsAccount inherits Account and implements InterestBearing. The account class is marked 'public sealed' to prevent unauthorized external subtyping.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
// Java: Basic OOP, Inheritance, and the 'final' keyword
class Animal {
    protected String name;
    public Animal(String name) { this.name = name; }
    public void speak() { System.out.println(name + " makes a sound."); }
}

class Dog extends Animal {
    public Dog(String name) { super(name); }
    @Override
    public void speak() { System.out.println(name + " barks: Woof!"); }
}

public class JavaSimpleDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Animal a = new Dog("Buddy");
        a.speak(); // Dynamic dispatch -> calls Dog.speak()
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
// Java: Interface Default Methods and Collision Resolution
interface Printer {
    default void show() { System.out.println("Standard Printer display"); }
}

interface Screen {
    default void show() { System.out.println("High-Res Screen display"); }
}

// Resolving diamond conflict between two default methods
class SmartKiosk implements Printer, Screen {
    @Override
    public void show() {
        // Explicitly disambiguate using InterfaceName.super.method()
        Screen.super.show();
        System.out.println("Kiosk combining screen and receipt printer.");
    }
}

public class JavaMediumDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        SmartKiosk kiosk = new SmartKiosk();
        kiosk.show();
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
// Java: Modern Sealed Classes (Java 17+) and Pattern Matching
sealed abstract class PaymentMethod permits CreditCard, UPI, Crypto {}

final class CreditCard extends PaymentMethod {
    public final String cardNumber;
    public CreditCard(String cardNumber) { this.cardNumber = cardNumber; }
}

final class UPI extends PaymentMethod {
    public final String vpa;
    public UPI(String vpa) { this.vpa = vpa; }
}

final class Crypto extends PaymentMethod {
    public final String walletAddress;
    public Crypto(String walletAddress) { this.walletAddress = walletAddress; }
}

public class JavaAdvancedDemo {
    public static void processPayment(PaymentMethod pm) {
        if (pm instanceof CreditCard cc) {
            System.out.println("Processing Credit Card: " + cc.cardNumber);
        } else if (pm instanceof UPI upi) {
            System.out.println("Processing UPI payment to: " + upi.vpa);
        } else if (pm instanceof Crypto c) {
            System.out.println("Processing Crypto to: " + c.walletAddress);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        PaymentMethod p1 = new UPI("user@okbank");
        PaymentMethod p2 = new CreditCard("4111-XXXX-XXXX-1111");
        processPayment(p1);
        processPayment(p2);
    }
}

RECORD_ID: OOP_LANG_CSHARP_001
CATEGORY: Language-Specific OOP Manuals
SUBCATEGORY: C# OOP Architecture
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C#
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C#, Java, C++, TypeScript
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Language Manual
QUESTION: How does C#'s Object-Oriented model work, including virtual/override/new, Properties, struct vs class, and Records?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Explain C# OOP features
virtual vs override vs new in C#
Difference between struct and class in C#
What are Properties and auto-properties in C#?
What are Records in C# 9+?
Value types vs Reference types in C#
C# mein OOP kaise kaam karta hai?
C# madhe OOP samjava
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
C#
.NET
virtual
override
new
method hiding
struct
class
properties
records
value types
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
C# features a statically typed, component-oriented OOP model running on the .NET CLR. Methods are non-virtual by default, requiring explicit 'virtual' in the base class and 'override' in the derived class (or 'new' for method hiding); encapsulates state via first-class Properties (with get, set, init accessors); distinguishes between stack-allocated Value Types (struct) and heap-allocated Reference Types (class); and supports immutable data modeling via 'record' types.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
C#'s OOP design combines the safety of Java with the expressiveness and performance of C++:
1. Explicit Polymorphism: Methods are non-virtual by default. To override a method, the base must mark it 'virtual' and the child must mark it 'override'. Marking a child method 'new' hides the base method without participating in runtime polymorphism.
2. Properties: First-class syntax for getters and setters (e.g., public double Balance { get; private set; }). C# 9 introduced 'init' accessors for immutable property initialization.
3. Struct vs Class: Classes are Reference Types allocated on the managed heap (garbage-collected, passed by reference). Structs are Value Types allocated inline on the stack or inside containing objects (passed by value, zero GC overhead).
4. Records (C# 9+): Reference types (or readonly record structs) featuring value-based equality, non-destructive mutation ('with' expression), and concise syntax.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Deep Technical Breakdown of C# and .NET Architecture:

1. Method Hiding vs Polymorphism (virtual/override vs new):
If a derived class declares a method with the same name as a base class method without 'override', the compiler issues a warning. Using the 'new' modifier suppresses the warning and introduces a new, independent method slot in the vtable. When invoked through a Base reference, the Base method executes (static binding); when invoked through a Derived reference, the Derived method executes.

2. Properties and Backing Fields:
Auto-properties (public string Name { get; set; }) automatically generate a private backing field. C# 11 introduced 'required' properties ensuring mandatory initialization.

3. Value Types (struct) vs Reference Types (class):
- Structs cannot inherit from other structs or classes (they implicitly inherit from System.ValueType).
- Structs avoid GC pressure, but passing large structs copies all bytes. C# provides 'in' and 'ref struct' (e.g., Span<T>) for zero-copy performance.
- Boxing and Unboxing: Converting a struct to an interface or object boxes it into a heap-allocated wrapper, incurring GC overhead.

4. Records and Value Equality:
Unlike standard classes where object identity (&a == &b) determines equality, Records automatically synthesize operator==, Equals(), and GetHashCode() based on field values.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine C# gives you two types of Lego bricks: a 'class' brick is like a remote-control car where you hand your friend the controller (reference); and a 'struct' brick is like a real solid wooden block where you hand your friend a brand new copy of the block. If your friend paints their block, yours stays clean!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
In C#, variables are wrapped in Properties. Instead of writing getAge() and setAge(), you just write Age. You can also decide if something is a 'class' (stored in big warehouse memory) or a 'struct' (stored right in your pocket memory for speed).
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
Think of student ID cards. If two students have ID cards with the exact same numbers and names, are they the same card? In normal classes, C# says 'No, they are two separate plastic cards'. In a Record, C# says 'Yes, the information on both cards is identical, so they are equal!'
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
C# requires explicit 'virtual' and 'override' keywords. Structs are value types; classes are reference types. Auto-properties simplify encapsulation. Records provide immutable data models with value-based equality.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Boxing and Unboxing Overhead: Assigning an int or struct to an object or interface (e.g., IComparable) allocates a box on the heap. Using generics (List<T>) avoids boxing completely, offering massive performance gains over old ArrayLists.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Ref Struct and Span<T>: 'ref struct' cannot be allocated on the heap, boxed, or used in async methods. This enables Span<T> to slice contiguous memory (stack, heap, or native arrays) with zero allocations and zero GC overhead.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
CLR Method Tables: In the .NET runtime, each type is backed by a MethodTable containing EEClass metadata, interface map, and slot table. 'override' overwrites the virtual slot in the base table; 'new' appends a brand new slot to the derived table.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
Financial Transaction Processing: Bank accounts are modeled as classes (reference identity matters), while monetary values are modeled as immutable structs (e.g., public readonly struct Money { decimal Amount; Currency Code; }). Daily statement snapshots are modeled as C# Records (record AccountStatement(DateTime Date, decimal Balance)).
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
// C#: Properties, virtual, override, and new in action
using System;

class BaseAccount {
    public virtual void CalculateFee() => Console.WriteLine("Base fee: $10");
    public void Terms() => Console.WriteLine("Standard Terms");
}

class PremiumAccount : BaseAccount {
    public override void CalculateFee() => Console.WriteLine("Discounted fee: $2");
    public new void Terms() => Console.WriteLine("VIP Concierge Terms"); // Method hiding
}

class Program {
    static void Main() {
        BaseAccount acc = new PremiumAccount();
        acc.CalculateFee(); // Virtual dispatch -> Discounted fee: $2
        acc.Terms();        // Static binding -> Standard Terms
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
// C#: Struct (Value Type) vs Class (Reference Type)
using System;

struct PointStruct {
    public int X, Y;
    public PointStruct(int x, int y) { X = x; Y = y; }
}

class PointClass {
    public int X, Y;
    public PointClass(int x, int y) { X = x; Y = y; }
}

class Program {
    static void Main() {
        PointStruct s1 = new PointStruct(10, 20);
        PointStruct s2 = s1; // Copies values!
        s2.X = 99;
        Console.WriteLine($"Struct s1.X: {s1.X}, s2.X: {s2.X}"); // s1 unchanged

        PointClass c1 = new PointClass(10, 20);
        PointClass c2 = c1; // Copies reference!
        c2.X = 99;
        Console.WriteLine($"Class c1.X: {c1.X}, c2.X: {c2.X}");   // c1 changed!
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
// C#: Modern Records (C# 9+) with Value Equality and 'with' expressions
using System;

public record Employee(string Name, string Role, decimal Salary);

class Program {
    static void Main() {
        Employee emp1 = new Employee("Alice", "Developer", 95000m);
        Employee emp2 = new Employee("Alice", "Developer", 95000m);

        // Value-based equality (True even though they are different heap instances!)
        Console.WriteLine($"Are emp1 and emp2 equal? {emp1 == emp2}");

        // Non-destructive mutation using 'with'
        Employee promoted = emp1 with { Role = "Lead Architect", Salary = 125000m };
        Console.WriteLine($"Original: {emp1}");
        Console.WriteLine($"Promoted: {promoted}");
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
Simple example: Demonstrates the crucial distinction between 'override' (virtual dynamic dispatch) and 'new' (method hiding with static binding). Medium example: Proves struct copy-by-value vs class copy-by-reference semantics. Advanced example: Demonstrates C# Records where emp1 == emp2 evaluates to True based on data equality, and the 'with' expression creates a clean immutable mutation.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
Discounted fee: $2
Standard Terms
[medium]
Struct s1.X: 10, s2.X: 99
Class c1.X: 99, c2.X: 99
[advanced]
Are emp1 and emp2 equal? True
Original: Employee { Name = Alice, Role = Developer, Salary = 95000 }
Promoted: Employee { Name = Alice, Role = Lead Architect, Salary = 125000 }
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT
COMMON_MISTAKES<<
Accidentally using 'new' instead of 'override', causing derived methods to be bypassed when accessed via a base class reference.
Creating large mutable structs (mutating a struct passed by value modifies only a temporary copy).
Boxing value types inside loops by casting them to object or interfaces, causing severe GC memory pressure.
Assuming classes compare by value like records do.

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_LANG_JAVASCRIPT_TS_001
CATEGORY: Language-Specific OOP Manuals
SUBCATEGORY: JavaScript & TypeScript OOP Architecture
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: JavaScript
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Dart
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Language Manual
QUESTION: How does Object-Oriented Programming work in JavaScript and TypeScript (Prototypes, ES6 Classes, #private fields, and Structural Typing)?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Explain JavaScript OOP and prototypes
What is the prototype chain in JavaScript?
ES6 classes vs Prototypes in JS
TypeScript private keyword vs JavaScript #private fields
Structural typing in TypeScript vs Nominal typing
JavaScript mein prototype kya hota hai?
JS and TS OOP tutorial
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
JavaScript
TypeScript
prototype
prototype chain
__proto__
ES6 class
#private
structural typing
duck typing
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
JavaScript implements prototype-based object orientation where objects inherit directly from other objects via a hidden [[Prototype]] link (__proto__); ES6 'class' syntax is syntactic sugar over prototype chains and constructor functions. TypeScript adds a compile-time static type system with structural typing ('duck typing at compile time'), interfaces, and access modifiers, while modern JavaScript (ES2022) introduces true runtime-enforced private fields using the '#' prefix.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
JavaScript's OOP differs fundamentally from class-based languages like C++ and Java:
1. Prototype Chain: Every JS object has a link to a prototype object. When an attribute or method is accessed (obj.foo), the JS engine searches obj, then obj.__proto__, and continues up the prototype chain until reaching Object.prototype, or null.
2. ES6 Classes: The 'class', 'extends', and 'super' keywords introduced in ES2015 provide clean class-like syntax, but under the hood, methods are still attached to ClassName.prototype.
3. Private Fields (#): Prior to ES2022, JavaScript had no true private fields. ES2022 added '#fieldName', which is strictly enforced at runtime by the JS engine.
4. TypeScript Structural Typing: In C++/Java (nominal typing), an object must explicitly inherit from a class or interface. In TypeScript (structural typing), if two objects share the same shape (attributes and methods), they are compatible, regardless of explicit declarations.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Deep Technical Breakdown of JS/TS Object Architecture:

1. Prototype Chain Mechanics:
When a function is declared, it receives a 'prototype' property (Foo.prototype = {}). When 'new Foo()' is executed:
- A brand new empty object is created.
- Its internal [[Prototype]] is linked to Foo.prototype.
- Foo is executed with 'this' bound to the new object.
- If Foo does not return an object, the newly created object is returned.

2. ES6 Class vs Prototypes:
Methods declared inside an ES6 class body are non-enumerable and defined on the prototype. Static methods are defined directly on the constructor function object itself. Super calls delegate to Object.getPrototypeOf(CurrentClass.prototype).

3. Privacy: TS 'private' vs JS '#':
- TypeScript 'private': Pure compile-time illusion. Erased completely during compilation to plain JavaScript (accessible via obj['secret']).
- JavaScript '#private': Hard runtime encapsulation. The engine stores private fields in PrivateEnvironment records; attempting to access obj.#secret outside the class triggers a SyntaxError.

4. TypeScript Type System:
TypeScript supports Interfaces, Type Aliases, Generics, Abstract Classes, and Utility Types. Its structural type system checks compatibility by property shape rather than class name hierarchy.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you want to build a paper plane. In Java, you must print out a blueprint first. In JavaScript, you just take your friend's existing paper plane and copy it, adding your own cool stickers! If someone asks your plane, 'Can you do a loop-the-loop?', your plane asks its parent plane, all the way back to the very first airplane ever folded!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
In JavaScript, objects can learn tricks from other objects directly. You can use the 'class' word to make things look familiar, but under the hood, JavaScript passes messages along an invisible family tree called the Prototype Chain.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
Think of electrical plugs and sockets. In TypeScript, if an object has the right prongs (shape), it plugs in! It doesn't matter if the plug says 'Sony' or 'Samsung'; as long as it has { voltage: 220, pins: 2 }, TypeScript accepts it. That is structural typing.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
JavaScript uses prototypal inheritance. ES6 classes compile to prototype assignments. TypeScript layers compile-time nominal-like syntax over a structural type system. Private fields (#) provide native runtime encapsulation.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
The 'this' binding trap: In JS, 'this' is dynamically bound based on call-site execution context (obj.method() binds this to obj, but const fn = obj.method; fn() binds this to undefined in strict mode). Arrow functions solve this by lexically binding 'this' from their enclosing scope.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
V8 Engine Hidden Classes and Inline Caches: The V8 JS engine compiles prototype lookups into C++ struct-like offsets using 'Hidden Classes' (Shapes/Maps). Mutating object properties in random order transitions hidden classes, de-optimizing the JIT compiler and slowing down method execution.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Prototype Pollution Vulnerability: In dynamic JS applications, unvalidated recursive object merging (like Object.assign(target, payload)) can overwrite Object.prototype properties, injecting malicious prototype properties across all objects in the runtime.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
Web UI Component Library: A BaseWidget class manages DOM rendering and event listeners. ButtonWidget and ModalWidget extend BaseWidget. TypeScript ensures component props match expected interfaces statically, while #domNode ensures internal HTML element references cannot be corrupted by external scripts.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
// JavaScript: ES6 Class with Runtime #private fields
class BankAccount {
    #balance = 0; // True runtime private field (ES2022)

    constructor(owner, initialBalance) {
        this.owner = owner;
        if (initialBalance > 0) this.#balance = initialBalance;
    }

    deposit(amount) {
        if (amount > 0) this.#balance += amount;
    }

    getBalance() {
        return this.#balance;
    }
}

const acc = new BankAccount("Priya", 500);
acc.deposit(200);
console.log(acc.owner + "'s Balance: $" + acc.getBalance());
// console.log(acc.#balance); // SyntaxError: Private field '#balance' must be declared in an enclosing class
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
// JavaScript: Prototype Chain Inheritance under the hood
function Animal(name) {
    this.name = name;
}

// Attaching method to prototype
Animal.prototype.speak = function() {
    return this.name + " makes a sound.";
};

function Dog(name, breed) {
    Animal.call(this, name); // Call super constructor
    this.breed = breed;
}

// Link prototype chain
Dog.prototype = Object.create(Animal.prototype);
Dog.prototype.constructor = Dog;

Dog.prototype.bark = function() {
    return this.name + " barks loudly!";
};

const d = new Dog("Sheru", "German Shepherd");
console.log(d.speak()); // Found on Animal.prototype!
console.log(d.bark());  // Found on Dog.prototype!
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
// TypeScript: Structural Typing, Interfaces, and Abstract Classes
abstract class Vehicle {
    abstract drive(): void;
}

interface Rechargeable {
    batteryLevel: number;
    charge(): void;
}

// Notice: Drone does NOT inherit from Rechargeable explicitly!
class Drone {
    batteryLevel: number = 85;
    charge(): void {
        console.log("Charging drone via USB-C to 100%...");
    }
}

function serviceDevice(device: Rechargeable) {
    console.log(`Current Battery: ${device.batteryLevel}%`);
    device.charge();
}

// Structural typing: Drone matches Rechargeable shape, so it compiles!
const myDrone = new Drone();
serviceDevice(myDrone);
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
Simple example (JS): Demonstrates modern ES6 class syntax with #balance, which cannot be accessed from outside the class even with hacky reflection. Medium example (JS): Illustrates how prototype chaining works under the hood via Object.create(Animal.prototype) and constructor stealing. Advanced example (TS): Demonstrates TypeScript's structural typing: Drone compiles cleanly into serviceDevice(device: Rechargeable) without an explicit 'implements Rechargeable' clause because its shape matches.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
Priya's Balance: $700

RECORD_ID: OOP_LANG_RUST_GO_001
CATEGORY: Language-Specific OOP Manuals
SUBCATEGORY: Non-Traditional OOP: Rust and Go
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: Multi-Language
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: Rust, Go, C++, Python
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Language Manual
QUESTION: How do Rust and Go support Object-Oriented principles (encapsulation, abstraction, polymorphism, composition) without conventional class-based inheritance?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Is Rust an Object-Oriented language?
Is Go an Object-Oriented language?
How does OOP work in Rust (traits and structs)?
How does OOP work in Go (interfaces and embedding)?
Why do Rust and Go reject class inheritance?
Traits in Rust vs Interfaces in Go
Rust aur Go mein OOP kaise hoti hai?
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
Rust
Go
Golang
traits
interfaces
struct embedding
composition
duck typing
dyn Trait
ownership
modern OOP
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Both Rust and Go deliberately reject classical class-based inheritance to eliminate the fragile base class and diamond problems, while fully supporting core OOP principles: data is encapsulated in structs, methods are attached via receivers/impl blocks, abstraction and polymorphism are realized through Traits (Rust) and implicit Interfaces (Go), and code reuse is achieved entirely through Composition and Struct Embedding.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Rust and Go represent modern post-class object orientation:
1. Rust: Decouples data (struct) from behavior. Methods are defined in 'impl StructName' blocks. Polymorphism is achieved via 'Traits' (defining shared interfaces). Rust supports both static dispatch (generics with trait bounds, monomorphized with zero runtime cost) and dynamic dispatch (trait objects: Box<dyn Trait> using fat pointers).
2. Go: Features lightweight structs and implicit interfaces. A struct satisfies an interface simply by implementing its method signatures—no 'implements' keyword exists. Code reuse is achieved via 'Struct Embedding' (composition with syntactic forwarding), which looks like inheritance but operates as composition.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Detailed Architecture of Rust and Go Non-Traditional OOP:

1. Rust OOP Paradigm:
- Encapsulation: Struct fields are private to their defining module by default; 'pub' keyword exposes fields.
- Polymorphism:
  * Static Dispatch: fn print_item<T: Display>(item: T) compiles separate monomorphized machine code for each type with zero overhead.
  * Dynamic Dispatch: fn print_item(item: &dyn Display) uses a 2-word fat pointer (data pointer + vtable pointer).
- No Inheritance: Rust has no subclassing. Code reuse is achieved through default trait methods, composition, and the 'Deref' coercion pattern.

2. Go OOP Paradigm:
- Methods with Receivers: Defined via func (r ReceiverType) MethodName(). Value receivers operate on a copy; Pointer receivers (*T) mutate the original struct.
- Implicit Interface Fulfillment: If type T implements all methods of interface I, T can be assigned to I directly. This enables post-hoc interface design where consumers define interfaces rather than library producers.
- Struct Embedding: A struct embedding another struct gains access to its fields and methods via automatic delegation. However, there is no virtual dispatch; calling an embedded method cannot polymorphically dispatch back to the outer struct (composition, not subclassing).
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine normal OOP is like a family tree where you inherit your parents' hair and eyes whether you like it or not. Rust and Go say: 'No family trees! You are a person (struct), and you can choose which superhero badges (traits or interfaces) you want to wear on your jacket!' It's cleaner, simpler, and nobody gets stuck with confusing family arguments.
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Instead of giant 'classes', Rust and Go keep things simple: you make a container for your data (struct), and then write functions that attach to that container. If you want two different things to be treated the same way, you define a list of rules called a Trait (Rust) or Interface (Go).
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In Go, you don't declare 'I implement this interface'. If an interface says 'Must be able to speak()', and your Robot struct has a speak() function, Go smiles and lets your Robot into the club automatically! In Rust, you explicitly write 'impl Speaker for Robot'.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Rust and Go favor composition over inheritance. Rust uses structs + traits + impl. Go uses structs + implicit interfaces + embedding. Both eliminate vtable inheritance trees while retaining polymorphism and abstraction.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Rust Trait Objects vs Monomorphization: Rust defaults to static dispatch (generics compile to dedicated inline assembly). When dynamic collections are needed (e.g. Vec<Box<dyn Shape>>), Rust uses dynamic dispatch with fat pointers (pointer to data + pointer to vtable).
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Go Interface Fat Pointers: An interface variable in Go occupies 2 machine words: (1) itable pointer (contains concrete type metadata and function pointers) and (2) data pointer. Assigning a concrete struct to an interface causes the runtime to synthesize or look up the itable dynamically.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Architectural Philosophy: Both languages reject the Fragile Base Class problem. Go optimizes for developer simplicity and rapid compilation; Rust optimizes for zero-cost abstractions and compile-time memory safety without a garbage collector.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
Cloud Microservices Logger: In Go or Rust, a service expects an abstract Logger interface with Log(msg string). You can pass a ConsoleLogger, FileLogger, or KafkaLogger interchangeably. The microservice doesn't care how the logger is built internally, achieving pure decoupled architecture without class hierarchies.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
// Go: Structs, Methods, and Implicit Interfaces
package main

import "fmt"

// Interface (No 'implements' keyword needed in Go!)
type Speaker interface {
    Speak() string
}

type Dog struct {
    Name string
}

// Method with pointer receiver
func (d *Dog) Speak() string {
    return d.Name + " says: Woof!"
}

func MakeItSpeak(s Speaker) {
    fmt.Println(s.Speak())
}

func main() {
    d := &Dog{Name: "Bruno"}
    MakeItSpeak(d) // Implicitly satisfies Speaker!
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
// Go: Struct Embedding (Composition simulating inheritance)
package main

import "fmt"

type Engine struct {
    Horsepower int
}

func (e *Engine) Start() {
    fmt.Printf("Engine started with %d HP.\n", e.Horsepower)
}

// Car embeds Engine (Composition, NOT inheritance!)
type Car struct {
    Brand string
    Engine // Embedded struct: methods are promoted
}

func main() {
    c := Car{
        Brand: "Ford",
        Engine: Engine{Horsepower: 300},
    }
    fmt.Println("Car brand:", c.Brand)
    c.Start() // Promoted method call from embedded Engine!
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
// Rust: Structs, Traits, Static vs Dynamic Dispatch
trait Shape {
    fn area(&self) -> f64;
}

struct Circle {
    radius: f64,
}

impl Shape for Circle {
    fn area(&self) -> f64 {
        std::f64::consts::PI * self.radius * self.radius
    }
}

struct Square {
    side: f64,
}

impl Shape for Square {
    fn area(&self) -> f64 {
        self.side * self.side
    }
}

// Static dispatch (compile-time monomorphization, zero overhead)
fn print_area_static<T: Shape>(shape: &T) {
    println!("Static dispatch area: {:.2}", shape.area());
}

fn main() {
    let c = Circle { radius: 3.0 };
    let s = Square { side: 4.0 };

    print_area_static(&c);
    print_area_static(&s);

    // Dynamic dispatch using trait objects (Box<dyn Shape>)
    let shapes: Vec<Box<dyn Shape>> = vec![
        Box::new(Circle { radius: 2.0 }),
        Box::new(Square { side: 5.0 }),
    ];

    for shape in shapes {
        println!("Dynamic dispatch area: {:.2}", shape.area());
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
Simple example (Go): Shows that Dog satisfies Speaker implicitly without writing 'implements Speaker'. Medium example (Go): Demonstrates Struct Embedding. Car embeds Engine, so c.Start() is promoted automatically via composition. Advanced example (Rust): Contrasts zero-cost static dispatch (<T: Shape>) with dynamic dispatch via trait objects (Box<dyn Shape>) over a heterogeneous vector.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
Bruno says: Woof!
[medium]
Car brand: Ford
Engine started with 300 HP.
[advanced]
Static dispatch area: 28.27
Static dispatch area: 16.00
Dynamic dispatch area: 12.57
Dynamic dispatch area: 25.00
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT

Assuming Go struct embedding is inheritance (there is no virtual dynamic dispatch back to the outer struct).
Confusing value receivers with pointer receivers in Go (value receivers cannot mutate struct state).
Attempting to construct a heterogeneous array of traits in Rust without 'dyn Trait' or Box.

RECORD_ID: OOP_DEBUG_TROUBLESHOOTING_001
CATEGORY: OOP Debugging and Misconceptions
SUBCATEGORY: Diagnostics and Antipatterns
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: Multi-Language
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python, JavaScript
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Troubleshooting
QUESTION: How do you diagnose and fix common OOP bugs and misconceptions (Object Slicing, Virtual Destructor leaks, Constructor Failures, and Fragile Base Class)?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is Object Slicing in C++?
Why is my destructor not being called?
Why can't I instantiate an abstract class?
Why is the wrong function being called in polymorphism?
What happens if an exception is thrown in a constructor?
How to fix memory leaks in OOP?
Common OOP bugs and how to fix them
Object slicing kya hota hai?
OOP debugging guide
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
debugging
object slicing
virtual destructor
constructor exception
diamond ambiguity
fragile base class
troubleshooting
pitfalls
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Common OOP bugs arise from subtle type-system and lifetime misconceptions: Object Slicing occurs when assigning a derived object to a base value variable (truncating derived members); Memory Leaks in polymorphic hierarchies occur when base class destructors lack the 'virtual' keyword; and Constructor Exceptions require careful resource handling because the destructor of an incompletely constructed object never runs.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Key diagnostic techniques for classic OOP failures:
1. Object Slicing (C++): Occurs when passing a Derived instance by value into a Base parameter (void func(Base b)). The Derived subobject is sliced off, leaving only Base members and slicing away the derived vtable. Fix: Pass by reference (const Base&) or pointer (Base*).
2. Destructor Not Called / Leak: When deleting a Derived object via a Base pointer (delete basePtr), if Base has a non-virtual destructor, undefined behavior ensues and the Derived destructor is skipped. Fix: Always declare virtual ~Base() = default; in polymorphic bases.
3. Calling Virtual Methods in Constructors: In C++, virtual calls in constructors do NOT dispatch to derived classes because the derived subobject is not yet constructed. In Java/C#, virtual calls do dispatch, but risk accessing uninitialized derived fields.
4. Constructor Exceptions: If a constructor throws, the object's destructor will NOT execute. Raw pointers allocated prior to the throw leak permanently unless managed by RAII smart pointers.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Deep Diagnostic Guide to Critical OOP Failure Modes:

1. The Object Slicing Bug (C++):
```cpp
class Base { public: int x; };
class Derived : public Base { public: int y; };
Derived d;
d.x = 1; d.y = 2;
Base b = d; // SLICING! 'y' is discarded, vtable pointer reset to Base!
```
Diagnostic: Derived attributes mysteriously vanish, or overridden virtual methods revert to base class behavior. Solution: Declare functions accepting references (Base&) or smart pointers (std::unique_ptr<Base>), and make base classes abstract.

2. The Non-Virtual Destructor Memory Leak:
When executing `Base* p = new Derived(); delete p;`, the compiler emits a static call to Base::~Base(). The Derived destructor body never executes, leaking any heap memory, sockets, or file locks acquired by Derived. Solution: Compiler warnings like -Wnon-virtual-dtor.

3. The Most Vexing Parse in C++:
`TimeKeeper tk();` does NOT create an object tk using the default constructor; it declares a function named tk that takes no parameters and returns a TimeKeeper! Fix: Use uniform brace initialization `TimeKeeper tk{};`.

4. Fragile Base Class Problem:
A seemingly innocuous modification to a base class (e.g., changing internal method calls) inadvertently breaks invariant assumptions in derived subclasses that override those methods. Solution: Prefer composition over inheritance, and use 'final' to seal non-extensible methods.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you have a big toy dump truck with a tipping bucket. If you try to force it into a small square toy box meant for a tiny car, the tipping bucket snaps right off! That is Object Slicing: trying to fit a big child object into a small parent box breaks off the extra parts!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
When you delete something in C++, if you didn't tell the parent class that it is 'virtual', the computer only cleans up the parent part and leaves the rest of the object rotting in memory. Always make sure your parent classes have a virtual destructor.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
Think of building a robot. If the robot's battery catches fire while you are halfway through assembling its legs (a constructor error), the robot never finished being built. The computer won't call the robot's dismantling routine. You must use smart parts (smart pointers) that clean themselves up if assembly fails.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Object slicing strips away derived data members when copying by value. Virtual destructors are mandatory for any class with virtual functions. In C++, virtual dispatch is inactive during construction and destruction.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Constructor Exception Safety: To satisfy the Basic and Strong Exception Guarantees, constructors must use the Member Initializer List with RAII types (e.g. std::string, std::unique_ptr) so that if member N throws, members 1 through N-1 are cleanly destroyed automatically.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Diamond Problem Ambiguity: When calling d.foo() in hybrid inheritance where B and C inherit non-virtually from A, the compiler aborts with 'request for member foo is ambiguous'. Resolving this requires virtual inheritance (class B : virtual public A), which forces D to construct the shared A subobject directly.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Undefined Behavior Sanitizers: Compilers cannot catch all polymorphic slicing and lifetime bugs at compile time. Production verification mandates runtime instrumentation using Clang/GCC AddressSanitizer (-fsanitize=address) and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (-fsanitize=undefined) to detect vptr corruption and heap use-after-free bugs.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
Database Connection Pool Crash: A DerivedConnection class holds an open OS TCP socket. A developer deletes the connection via an IDbConnection* base pointer whose destructor was not marked virtual. The base destructor runs, but the Derived destructor is skipped. The TCP socket remains in CLOSE_WAIT state indefinitely. Under heavy load, the server exhausts OS file descriptors and crashes entirely.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
// C++: Object Slicing and Virtual Destructor Diagnostic
#include <iostream>

class Base {
public:
    virtual ~Base() { std::cout << "~Base() called.\n"; }
    virtual void describe() const { std::cout << "I am Base.\n"; }
};

class Derived : public Base {
public:
    int extraData = 99;
    ~Derived() override { std::cout << "~Derived() called.\n"; }
    void describe() const override { std::cout << "I am Derived with extraData = " << extraData << ".\n"; }
};

void passByValue(Base b) { // SLICING OCCURS HERE!
    b.describe();
}

void passByRef(const Base& b) { // CORRECT: Polymorphism preserved
    b.describe();
}

int main() {
    Derived d;
    std::cout << "--- Pass by Value (Sliced): ---\n";
    passByValue(d); // Slices Derived into Base

    std::cout << "--- Pass by Reference (Polymorphic): ---\n";
    passByRef(d);
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
# Python: Diagnosing Mutable Default Arguments in __init__
class BadUserList:
    # BUG: items=[] is evaluated once at function definition time!
    def __init__(self, items=[]):
        self.items = items

class GoodUserList:
    # FIX: Use None as default and instantiate inside __init__
    def __init__(self, items=None):
        self.items = [] if items is None else items

print("--- Testing Buggy Class (Shared Mutable State): ---")
u1 = BadUserList()
u1.items.append("Admin")
u2 = BadUserList() # Unexpectedly contains 'Admin' from u1!
print("u2.items:", u2.items)

print("--- Testing Fixed Class (Independent State): ---")
g1 = GoodUserList()
g1.items.append("Admin")
g2 = GoodUserList()
print("g2.items:", g2.items) # Clean and empty!
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
// Java: Calling Virtual Methods in Constructors Pitfall
class BaseAccount {
    public BaseAccount() {
        System.out.println("Base constructor running...");
        init(); // DANGEROUS: Invokes derived override before derived constructor runs!
    }
    public void init() {
        System.out.println("Base init");
    }
}

class CryptoAccount extends BaseAccount {
    private String walletAddress = "INITIALIZING_DEFAULT";

    public CryptoAccount(String addr) {
        // Implicit super() runs first!
        this.walletAddress = addr;
        System.out.println("Crypto constructor running: wallet set to " + walletAddress);
    }

    @Override
    public void init() {
        // BUG: walletAddress is NULL here because field initialization has not finished!
        System.out.println("Crypto init running! walletAddress is: " + walletAddress);
    }
}

public class ConstructorPitfallDemo {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        CryptoAccount acc = new CryptoAccount("0x71C...49A");
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
Simple example (C++): Demonstrates Object Slicing when passByValue(Base b) truncates Derived d, causing describe() to print 'I am Base'. In contrast, passByRef(const Base& b) maintains dynamic dispatch. Medium example (Python): Diagnoses the classic mutable default argument trap in __init__, demonstrating how u2 shares u1's list and showing the idiomatic 'items=None' fix. Advanced example (Java): Shows the dangerous flaw of calling virtual methods inside constructors. In Java, init() dispatches to CryptoAccount before walletAddress is assigned, printing null and risking NullPointerExceptions.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION

LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Token preprocessing pipeline: lowercase -> strip punctuation -> n-gram generation -> alias dictionary lookup -> BM25 ranking over canonical knowledge base records.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
In computational linguistics, this provides an inverted index synonym lexicon coupled with phonetic hashing (Double Metaphone / Soundex adapted for Indic transliterated queries) to maximize chatbot retrieval recall.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A student in Pune types into the website: 'bhai class ani object madhla farak kay ahe sanga na'. The normalizer extracts 'class ani object madhla farak', maps it to canonical key 'OOP_CPP_CLASS_OBJECT_001', and returns the Marathi detailed explanation.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
// C++: Token Normalization and Alias Lookup Demonstration
#include <iostream>
#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <algorithm>

class QueryNormalizer {
private:
    std::unordered_map<std::string, std::string> aliasMap;
public:
    QueryNormalizer() {
        // Common typos
        aliasMap["inhertance"] = "INHERITANCE";
        aliasMap["polymorphisam"] = "POLYMORPHISM";
        aliasMap["encapulation"] = "ENCAPSULATION";
        aliasMap["constructer"] = "CONSTRUCTOR";
        
        // Hinglish queries
        aliasMap["inheritance kya hai"] = "INHERITANCE";
        aliasMap["class aur object mein difference"] = "CLASS_OBJECT";
        
        // Marathish queries
        aliasMap["encapsulation mhanje kay"] = "ENCAPSULATION";
        aliasMap["class ani object madhla farak"] = "CLASS_OBJECT";
    }

    std::string normalize(const std::string& rawQuery) {
        std::string q = rawQuery;
        std::transform(q.begin(), q.end(), q.begin(), ::tolower);
        for (const auto& pair : aliasMap) {
            if (q.find(pair.first) != std::string::npos) {
                return pair.second;
            }
        }
        return "UNKNOWN_TOPIC";
    }
};

int main() {
    QueryNormalizer normalizer;
    std::cout << "Query: 'inhertance in c++' -> Canonical: " 
              << normalizer.normalize("inhertance in c++") << "\n";
    std::cout << "Query: 'class ani object madhla farak kay' -> Canonical: " 
              << normalizer.normalize("class ani object madhla farak kay") << "\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
# Python: Query Tokenizer and Transliteration Normalizer
NORMALIZATION_DICT = {
    "inhertance": "INHERITANCE",
    "polymorphisam": "POLYMORPHISM",
    "encapulation": "ENCAPSULATION",
    "constructer": "CONSTRUCTOR",
    "inheritance kya hai": "INHERITANCE",
    "class aur object mein difference": "CLASS_OBJECT",
    "encapsulation mhanje kay": "ENCAPSULATION",
    "class ani object madhla farak": "CLASS_OBJECT",
}

def normalize_query(query: str) -> str:
    q_clean = query.lower().strip()
    for phrase, canonical in NORMALIZATION_DICT.items():
        if phrase in q_clean:
            return canonical
    return "GENERAL_OOP"

print("Input: 'mujhe polymorphisam samjhao' ->", normalize_query("mujhe polymorphisam samjhao"))
print("Input: 'class ani object madhla farak kay ahe' ->", normalize_query("class ani object madhla farak kay ahe"))
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
// Java: Levenshtein Distance & Phonetic Fuzzy Matcher for Typo Tolerance
public class FuzzyMatcher {
    public static int levenshtein(String a, String b) {
        int[] costs = new int[b.length() + 1];
        for (int j = 0; j < costs.length; j++) costs[j] = j;
        for (int i = 1; i <= a.length(); i++) {
            costs[0] = i;
            int nw = i - 1;
            for (int j = 1; j <= b.length(); j++) {
                int cj = Math.min(1 + Math.min(costs[j], costs[j - 1]),
                        a.charAt(i - 1) == b.charAt(j - 1) ? nw : nw + 1);
                nw = costs[j];
                costs[j] = cj;
            }
        }
        return costs[b.length()];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String canonical = "inheritance";
        String[] userInputs = {"inhertance", "inheretance", "inheeritance", "polymorphism"};
        for (String input : userInputs) {
            int dist = levenshtein(canonical, input);
            boolean match = dist <= 2;
            System.out.println("Input: '" + input + "' vs '" + canonical + "' -> Dist: " + dist + ", Match: " + match);
        }
    }
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE

Medium example (Python): Demonstrates substring extraction over user search text. Advanced example (Java): Implements the Levenshtein distance algorithm, proving how typos with edit distance <= 2 (e.g. 'inhertance') are automatically matched to 'inheritance'.

RECORD_ID: OOP_INHERITANCE_MULTILEVEL_001
CATEGORY: Inheritance
SUBCATEGORY: Multilevel Inheritance
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python, TypeScript, Swift, Kotlin
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Concept and Implementation
QUESTION: What is multilevel inheritance in C++, and how do constructors and destructors execute across the inheritance chain?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is multilevel inheritance?
Explain multilevel inheritance in C++ with an example
How does multilevel inheritance work?
Multilevel inheritance constructor and destructor execution order
Multilevel inheritance chain in C++
Grandparent parent child inheritance in C++
Multilevel inheritance syntax and example
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
multilevel inheritance
multilevel
inheritance chain
grandparent parent child
constructor execution order
destructor execution order
derived class of derived class
class hierarchy
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Multilevel inheritance is an inheritance mechanism where a class is derived from another derived class, creating a linear chain of inheritance (e.g., Class C inherits from Class B, which in turn inherits from Class A). The derived class at the bottom inherits non-private members from all ancestor classes above it.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
In C++ multilevel inheritance, a class acts as a derived class to its predecessor and as a base class to its successor. For example, in the hierarchy `Device -> Computer -> Laptop`, `Laptop` inherits directly from `Computer` and indirectly from `Device`. `Laptop` gains access to public and protected members of both `Computer` and `Device`. When a `Laptop` object is created, constructors execute in top-down order from the base down to the most derived class (`Device` -> `Computer` -> `Laptop`). When the object is destroyed, destructors execute in reverse bottom-up order (`Laptop` -> `Computer` -> `Device`).
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Multilevel inheritance forms a vertical lineage of types in object-oriented programming. In C++, each level of the hierarchy introduces its own subobject within the final derived object's memory layout.

1. Memory Layout and Member Access:
An object of the most-derived class contains subobjects for every ancestor class. Non-private members propagate down the chain according to the access specifier used at each derivation step. If public inheritance is used throughout (`class B : public A` and `class C : public B`), public members of A remain public in C, protected members of A remain protected in C, and private members of A remain completely private and inaccessible directly to C.

2. Constructor and Destructor Execution Order:
- Construction Order: Ancestor constructors are executed strictly before derived class constructors. In `A -> B -> C`, constructor `A()` executes first, followed by `B()`, followed by `C()`.
- Constructor Chaining: Each derived class constructor invokes its immediate base constructor through its member initializer list: `Laptop(string brand, int ram) : Computer(brand, ram) { ... }`.
- Destruction Order: Destructors execute in exact reverse order of construction (`~C()` -> `~B()` -> `~A()`). If polymorphic deletion is expected via a pointer to `A`, the base destructor in `A` MUST be declared `virtual`.

3. Cross-Language Differences:
- C++: Supports unrestricted multilevel inheritance with stack and heap allocation.
- Java & C#: Support multilevel inheritance for classes, rooted at `java.lang.Object` or `System.Object`.
- Python: Uses method resolution order (MRO) with C3 linearization to traverse multilevel chains via `super()`.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Think of multilevel inheritance like a family recipe passed down through generations! Your grandmother creates a secret pasta sauce recipe (Grandparent class). Your mother inherits that recipe and adds fresh garlic bread to the dinner (Parent class). Then you inherit everything—the pasta sauce and the garlic bread—and add chocolate cake for dessert (Child class). You get all the goodies from the entire family chain!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Multilevel inheritance means one class inherits from another class that is ALREADY inherited from a base class.
Class A is the Base class.
Class B inherits from Class A (B is a child of A).
Class C inherits from Class B (C is a child of B, and grandchild of A).
Class C gets all public features of both Class A and Class B.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In school terms:
Level 1: Person (has name, age)
Level 2: Student inherits from Person (adds rollNumber, schoolName)
Level 3: CollegeStudent inherits from Student (adds major, semester)
CollegeStudent has name, age, rollNumber, schoolName, major, and semester!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
In C++, syntax is:
class Device { ... };
class Computer : public Device { ... };
class Laptop : public Computer { ... };
Remember constructor order: Device() -> Computer() -> Laptop().
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Always initialize immediate base classes in the constructor member initializer list. For example, `Laptop(...) : Computer(...)` delegates initialization to `Computer`, which in turn delegates to `Device`.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
When using base pointers in a multilevel hierarchy (`Device* d = new Laptop()`), ensure `Device` has a virtual destructor (`virtual ~Device() = default;`). Otherwise, `delete d;` will only invoke `~Device()`, skipping `~Laptop()` and `~Computer()` and leaking resources.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
In the ABI memory model (Itanium C++ ABI), the layout of `Laptop` contains the `Device` subobject, followed by `Computer` data members, followed by `Laptop` data members. If virtual functions are introduced at any level, the vptr is initialized to point to that level's vtable during construction and updated as derived constructors run.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
In a graphics rendering engine or UI system:
Level 1: UIElement (bounds, visibility, draw())
Level 2: Panel : public UIElement (layout manager, children list)
Level 3: ScrollablePanel : public Panel (scrollbars, scroll offsets)
ScrollablePanel is a Panel and also a UIElement.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

// Level 1: Base Class
class Device {
public:
    void powerOn() const {
        std::cout << "Device powered on.\n";
    }
};

// Level 2: Intermediate Derived Class
class Computer : public Device {
public:
    void bootOS() const {
        std::cout << "Operating system booted.\n";
    }
};

// Level 3: Leaf Derived Class
class Laptop : public Computer {
public:
    void closeLid() const {
        std::cout << "Laptop lid closed, entering sleep.\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    Laptop myLaptop;
    myLaptop.powerOn();   // Inherited from Device
    myLaptop.bootOS();    // Inherited from Computer
    myLaptop.closeLid();  // Defined in Laptop
    return 0;
}

class Vehicle {

    std::string brand;

        std::cout << "Vehicle destructor: " << brand << "\n";

    int numDoors;

        std::cout << "Car destructor\n";

        std::cout << "ElectricCar destructor\n";

        std::cout << "Brand: " << brand << ", Doors: " << numDoors

    }

    ElectricCar tesla("Tesla", 4, 100);

class Component {

    virtual ~Component() { std::cout << "1. Component destroyed\n"; }

    ~StorageComponent() override { std::cout << "2. StorageComponent destroyed\n"; }

    void inspect() const override { std::cout << "NVMeDrive PCI-e Gen 4 inspect\n"; }
    ~NVMeDrive() override { std::cout << "3. NVMeDrive destroyed\n"; }
};

int main() {
    std::unique_ptr<Component> dev = std::make_unique<NVMeDrive>();
    dev->inspect();
    // Destructor runs polymorphically across all 3 levels in reverse order
    return 0;
}

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_INHERITANCE_MULTIPLE_001
CATEGORY: Inheritance
SUBCATEGORY: Multiple Inheritance
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Python, Eiffel
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Concept and Implementation
QUESTION: What is multiple inheritance in C++, how is it declared, and how does it handle ambiguity?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is multiple inheritance?
Explain multiple inheritance in C++
Multiple base classes in C++
How does multiple inheritance work?
Multiple inheritance syntax and example
Why does Java not support multiple inheritance with classes?
Multiple inheritance ambiguity resolution
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
multiple inheritance
multiple base classes
comma separated base classes
ambiguity resolution
scope resolution operator
diamond problem
C++ multiple inheritance
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Multiple inheritance is a feature in C++ where a class can inherit simultaneously from more than one direct base class (`class Derived : public Base1, public Base2`). It allows a derived class to combine features from multiple distinct types, but requires careful handling of member name ambiguity.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Multiple inheritance allows a single derived class to have multiple immediate parent classes. For example, `class FlyingCar : public Car, public Airplane` inherits both driving features from `Car` and flight features from `Airplane`. If both base classes define a member with the identical name (e.g., `start()`), calling `flyingCar.start()` causes a compiler error due to ambiguity. This ambiguity must be resolved explicitly using the scope resolution operator (`flyingCar.Car::start()`) or by overriding the method in the derived class.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Multiple inheritance is supported in C++, Python, and Eiffel, while intentionally excluded for concrete classes in Java, C#, and Dart to prevent complexity.

1. Declaration Syntax:
`class Derived : public Base1, public Base2 { ... };`

2. Constructor and Destructor Execution Order:
- Base class constructors execute in the EXACT order they appear in the class declaration list (`Base1`, then `Base2`), regardless of the order in the constructor's member initializer list.
- Destructors execute in reverse order (`~Derived()`, then `~Base2()`, then `~Base1()`).

3. Memory Layout:
An object of `Derived` contains the subobject for `Base1` at offset 0, followed immediately by the subobject for `Base2` at a non-zero offset. When casting a `Derived*` to a `Base2*`, the compiler automatically adjusts the pointer address by the byte offset of `Base2`.

4. Ambiguity Resolution:
If both `Base1` and `Base2` declare `void display()`, calling `d.display()` fails to compile (`request for member 'display' is ambiguous`). Resolution methods:
- Explicit qualification: `d.Base1::display()` or `d.Base2::display()`
- Override in derived class: Implement `Derived::display()` and explicitly delegate to one or both base versions.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine a smartphone! A smartphone inherits from a Camera (it takes pictures) and also inherits from a Phone (it makes calls). It has two parents! That is multiple inheritance: one kid having two parents and getting talents from both.
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
In single inheritance, a class has ONE parent. In multiple inheritance, a class has TWO OR MORE parents:
`class SmartPhone : public Camera, public Phone { };`
The SmartPhone gets features from both Camera and Phone.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In school: A TeacherAssistant is both a Student (enrolls in classes, takes exams) and an Employee (receives a salary, grades papers). So:
`class TeacherAssistant : public Student, public Employee { };`
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Notice that constructor order depends on the declaration line:
`class C : public A, public B` -> A constructor runs first, then B constructor!
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Beware of duplicate method names. If both `A` and `B` have `print()`, you must call `obj.A::print()` or `obj.B::print()`.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Pointer arithmetic occurs during upcasting. If `Base2` is the second base in `class Derived : public Base1, public Base2`, then `Base2* b2 = &derived;` actually adjusts the pointer value by `sizeof(Base1)`.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Multiple inheritance can lead to the Diamond Problem if both `Base1` and `Base2` share an ancestor `Root`. Use `virtual public Root` in both `Base1` and `Base2` to ensure a single shared `Root` subobject.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A multimedia player device:
`class AudioPlayer { public: void playAudio(); };`
`class VideoPlayer { public: void playVideo(); };`
`class MediaPlayer : public AudioPlayer, public VideoPlayer { };`
MediaPlayer can play both audio files and video files.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Printer {
public:
    void printDocument() const {
        std::cout << "Printing document...\n";
    }
};

class Scanner {
public:
    void scanDocument() const {
        std::cout << "Scanning document...\n";
    }
};

// Multiple Inheritance
class Copier : public Printer, public Scanner {
public:
    void copyDocument() const {
        scanDocument();
        printDocument();
    }
};

int main() {
    Copier copier;
    copier.printDocument(); // from Printer
    copier.scanDocument();  // from Scanner
    copier.copyDocument();  // defined in Copier
    return 0;
}

EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class GPS {
public:
    void navigate() const { std::cout << "GPS: Navigating route.\n"; }
    void reportStatus() const { std::cout << "GPS: Status OK.\n"; }
};

class Radio {
public:
    void tuneStation(double freq) const { std::cout << "Radio: Tuned to " << freq << " MHz.\n"; }
    void reportStatus() const { std::cout << "Radio: Signal strong.\n"; }
};

class InfotainmentSystem : public GPS, public Radio {
public:
    // Resolving ambiguous method name
    void reportStatus() const {
        std::cout << "--- Combined System Status ---\n";
        GPS::reportStatus();
        Radio::reportStatus();
    }
};

int main() {
    InfotainmentSystem sys;
    sys.navigate();
    sys.tuneStation(98.5);
    sys.reportStatus(); // Calls resolved method
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Base1 {
public:
    int x = 10;
    virtual void show() { std::cout << "Base1::show, x=" << x << "\n"; }
    virtual ~Base1() = default;
};

class Base2 {
public:
    int y = 20;
    virtual void display() { std::cout << "Base2::display, y=" << y << "\n"; }
    virtual ~Base2() = default;
};

class Derived : public Base1, public Base2 {
public:
    int z = 30;
    void show() override { std::cout << "Derived::show, z=" << z << "\n"; }
    void display() override { std::cout << "Derived::display\n"; }
};

int main() {
    Derived d;
    Base1* p1 = &d;
    Base2* p2 = &d; // Pointer adjustment happens here automatically!
    std::cout << "Address of d:  " << (void*)&d << "\n";
    std::cout << "Address in p1: " << (void*)p1 << "\n";
    std::cout << "Address in p2: " << (void*)p2 << "\n";
    p1->show();
    p2->display();
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE

The simple example shows a Copier inheriting from Printer and Scanner. The medium example resolves the ambiguous `reportStatus()` method shared by both GPS and Radio. The advanced example proves that pointer adjustment occurs when casting to the second base class pointer.

RECORD_ID: OOP_INHERITANCE_HIERARCHICAL_001
CATEGORY: Inheritance
SUBCATEGORY: Hierarchical Inheritance
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python, TypeScript, Swift, Kotlin
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Beginner
QUESTION_TYPE: Concept and Implementation
QUESTION: What is hierarchical inheritance in C++, and how does it organize classes?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is hierarchical inheritance?
Explain hierarchical inheritance in C++
Hierarchical inheritance with example
One base class multiple derived classes
Hierarchical inheritance tree structure
Explain hierarchical inheritance in Marathi
Explain hierarchical inheritance in Hindi
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
hierarchical inheritance
hierarchical
tree structure
one parent multiple children
single base multiple derived
sibling classes
Shape hierarchy
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Hierarchical inheritance is an inheritance structure where multiple derived classes inherit from a single common base class. Each derived class shares the common attributes and behaviors of the base class while remaining mutually independent of its sibling derived classes.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
In hierarchical inheritance, one base class acts as the root for many derived classes, creating an inverted tree structure. For example, `class Shape` can serve as the base class for `Circle`, `Rectangle`, and `Triangle`. All three derived classes inherit common properties (such as color or position) and methods (such as `draw()` or `area()`), but each provides its own specialized implementation. The sibling classes (`Circle` and `Rectangle`) do not inherit from each other and cannot access each other's members.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Hierarchical inheritance is the most common structural pattern in class libraries and GUI frameworks.

1. Class Relationships:
- Root Base: Contains shared state and common interface contract.
- Child Classes: Derive publicly from the root base and specialize behavior.
- Sibling Independence: Sibling classes are completely decoupled. Modifying `Circle` has zero effect on `Rectangle`.

2. Polymorphic Use Cases:
Hierarchical inheritance is the foundation of subtype polymorphism:
```cpp
std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> shapes;
shapes.push_back(std::make_unique<Circle>(5.0));
shapes.push_back(std::make_unique<Rectangle>(4.0, 6.0));
for (const auto& s : shapes) s->draw();
```
A single collection of base pointers can hold any combination of sibling types.

3. Cross-Language Comparisons:
- C++: Directly supported.
- Java: Standard class hierarchies (`java.lang.Number` -> `Integer`, `Double`, `Float`).
- C#: Standard framework structure (`System.IO.Stream` -> `FileStream`, `MemoryStream`).
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Think of a tree branch! The main branch is Animal. Growing out of it are different smaller branches: Dog, Cat, and Bird! All of them are Animals, but a Dog doesn't meow like a Cat, and a Cat doesn't fly like a Bird. They all share the Animal tree trunk!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
One parent has multiple children.
Base class: Account (balance, deposit)
Derived 1: SavingsAccount (has interestRate)
Derived 2: CheckingAccount (has overdraftLimit)
Both inherit from Account, but they are separate from each other.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In a school app: User is the parent class. Teacher, Student, and Principal all inherit from User. They all have names and login credentials, but distinct roles.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Hierarchical inheritance allows code reuse without code duplication: shared attributes like `name` and `id` are written once in the base class.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Use pure virtual functions in the base class if each sibling class must provide its own specific formula (like `area()`).
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Avoid putting derived-specific logic into the base class. Sibling classes should adhere to the Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP).
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Hierarchical trees are prone to deep inheritance issues if developers prematurely add intermediate levels. Prefer composition when behaviors vary across dimensions not captured by the primary hierarchy.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
In a banking system:
`Account` (base: balance, accountNumber)
-> `SavingsAccount` : public Account (adds interest calculation)
-> `CheckingAccount` : public Account (adds overdraft fee checks)
-> `FixedDepositAccount` : public Account (adds lock-in maturity date)
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

// Single Base Class
class Animal {
public:
    void eat() const {
        std::cout << "This animal eats food.\n";
    }
};

// Derived Class 1
class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    void bark() const {
        std::cout << "Dog barks: Woof!\n";
    }
};

// Derived Class 2
class Cat : public Animal {
public:
    void meow() const {
        std::cout << "Cat meows: Meow!\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    Dog dog;
    Cat cat;
    dog.eat();  // Inherited from Animal
    dog.bark(); // Dog-specific

    cat.eat();  // Inherited from Animal
    cat.meow(); // Cat-specific
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <memory>

class Shape {
protected:
    std::string name;
public:
    explicit Shape(const std::string& n) : name(n) {}
    virtual double area() const = 0; // Pure virtual
    virtual void print() const {
        std::cout << name << " with area: " << area() << "\n";
    }
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
};

class Circle : public Shape {
    double radius;
public:
    Circle(double r) : Shape("Circle"), radius(r) {}
    double area() const override { return 3.14159 * radius * radius; }
};

class Rectangle : public Shape {
    double width, height;
public:
    Rectangle(double w, double h) : Shape("Rectangle"), width(w), height(h) {}
    double area() const override { return width * height; }
};

int main() {
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Shape>> shapes;
    shapes.push_back(std::make_unique<Circle>(3.0));
    shapes.push_back(std::make_unique<Rectangle>(4.0, 5.0));

    for (const auto& s : shapes) {
        s->print();
    }
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>
#include <memory>

class Employee {
protected:
    std::string name;
    double baseSalary;
public:
    Employee(const std::string& n, double s) : name(n), baseSalary(s) {}
    virtual double calculateBonus() const = 0;
    virtual void displayInfo() const {
        std::cout << "Employee: " << name << " | Base: $" << baseSalary 
                  << " | Bonus: $" << calculateBonus() << "\n";
    }
    virtual ~Employee() = default;
};

class SoftwareEngineer : public Employee {
    int stockUnits;
public:
    SoftwareEngineer(const std::string& n, double s, int stocks) 
        : Employee(n, s), stockUnits(stocks) {}
    double calculateBonus() const override { return baseSalary * 0.15 + (stockUnits * 10); }
};

class SalesExecutive : public Employee {
    double totalSales;
public:
    SalesExecutive(const std::string& n, double s, double sales) 
        : Employee(n, s), totalSales(sales) {}
    double calculateBonus() const override { return baseSalary * 0.05 + (totalSales * 0.08); }
};

int main() {
    std::unique_ptr<Employee> e1 = std::make_unique<SoftwareEngineer>("Maya", 120000, 500);
    std::unique_ptr<Employee> e2 = std::make_unique<SalesExecutive>("Raj", 80000, 250000);
    e1->displayInfo();
    e2->displayInfo();
    return 0;
}
