=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_INHERITANCE_HYBRID_DIAMOND_001
CATEGORY: Inheritance
SUBCATEGORY: Hybrid Inheritance and The Diamond Problem
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Python (via MRO), Eiffel
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Deep Dive Problem and Solution
QUESTION: What is hybrid inheritance, what is the diamond problem, and how does virtual inheritance solve it in C++?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Explain hybrid inheritance with an example
What is the diamond problem in C++?
How does virtual inheritance solve the diamond problem?
What is a virtual base class in C++?
Diamond problem resolution in C++
Why do we need virtual inheritance?
Explain diamond problem and hybrid inheritance
Diamond problem kya hai
Diamond problem mhanje kay
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
hybrid inheritance
diamond problem
virtual inheritance
virtual base class
virtual public
ambiguity
multiple paths
shared subobject
dreaded diamond
vptr offset
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Hybrid inheritance combines two or more inheritance types (e.g., hierarchical and multiple). The Diamond Problem is a classic ambiguity that occurs when a derived class inherits from two intermediate classes that share a common base class, resulting in duplicate base subobjects; C++ solves this using `virtual` inheritance (`class B : virtual public A`), ensuring only one shared instance of the common base class exists.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Hybrid inheritance occurs when multiple inheritance structures are combined. In the classic Diamond Problem, class `A` is the root base. Classes `B` and `C` both inherit from `A` (hierarchical). Class `D` then inherits from both `B` and `C` (multiple). Without virtual inheritance, an object of `D` contains two complete copies of `A`: one through `B` and one through `C`. Accessing a member `d.x` results in a compilation error: `request for member 'x' is ambiguous`. C++ resolves this via **virtual base classes**: by declaring `class B : virtual public A` and `class C : virtual public A`, the compiler ensures only one shared `A` subobject is allocated inside `D`, resolving ambiguity and eliminating redundant state.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
The Diamond Problem (also known as the 'Deadly Diamond of Death') is the primary historical critique of multiple inheritance. C++ resolves it through virtual base classes.

1. The Problem Anatomy (Without Virtual Inheritance):
```
      A (int data)
     / \
    B   C
     \ /
      D
```
- Memory layout of `D`: `[ A subobject (via B) | B members | A subobject (via C) | C members | D members ]`
- Duplication: `sizeof(D)` includes two `A` subobjects.
- Ambiguity: `D d; d.data = 5;` -> COMPILER ERROR: `member 'data' is ambiguous`.
- Unsafe workaround: Explicit qualification `d.B::data = 5; d.C::data = 10;`, leaving data in an inconsistent state.

2. The Solution: Virtual Inheritance (`class B : virtual public A`, `class C : virtual public A`):
- When virtual base classes are used, `B` and `C` do not embed `A` at fixed offsets. Instead, they store a pointer/offset (via the vtable or virtual base pointer `vbptr`) to a shared `A` subobject.
- Memory layout of `D`: `[ B members + vptr | C members + vptr | D members | Single shared A subobject ]`
- Unambiguous: `d.data = 5;` compiles cleanly and modifies the single shared `A` subobject.

3. Constructor Responsibilities in Virtual Inheritance:
In non-virtual inheritance, each derived class initializes its immediate base. In virtual inheritance, the **most-derived class** (`D`) is solely responsible for initializing the virtual base class (`A`). Intermediate classes `B` and `C` skip their calls to `A`'s constructor when constructed as part of `D`.
Syntax in D:
`D(int val) : A(val), B(), C() { ... }`

4. Cross-Language Comparisons:
- C++: Solves it via explicit virtual base classes.
- Python: Uses C3 Linearization Method Resolution Order (MRO); each class in the diamond is visited once.
- Java & C#: Do not allow multiple inheritance of state/classes; default interface methods resolve via explicit overrides.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine a grandfather gives a family treasure chest to both your father and your uncle. If both of them try to pass the treasure chest down to you, you might end up with two confusing chests that aren't the same! With virtual inheritance, everyone agrees there is only ONE real treasure chest in the family room that everyone shares.
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Hybrid inheritance is a mix of two types of inheritance. The diamond problem happens when two parents share the same grandparent, and pass two copies of the grandparent's variables to the child. To fix it in C++, just add the word `virtual`:
`class B : virtual public A`
`class C : virtual public A`
Now the child only gets ONE copy!
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In school: A teaching assistant is a Person. Student is a Person. Teacher is a Person. TeachingAssistant inherits from both Student and Teacher. Without `virtual`, the assistant has two names and two birthdates in memory! With `virtual public Person`, there is only one name and one birthdate.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
In diamond hierarchies:
`class B : virtual public A { ... };`
`class C : virtual public A { ... };`
`class D : public B, public C { ... };`
In class `D`'s constructor, YOU must call `A()` directly: `D() : A(), B(), C() {}`.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Notice `sizeof(D)`: Without virtual inheritance, `sizeof(D)` is larger because it duplicates `A`. With virtual inheritance, it contains one `A`, plus virtual pointer overhead (`vptr`/`vbptr`).
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
The most-derived class initializes the virtual base. If you construct `B` alone, `B` initializes `A`. But when constructing `D`, `D` initializes `A`, and `B`'s call to `A`'s constructor is completely ignored by the runtime!
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Under the Itanium C++ ABI, the offset to the virtual base subobject is stored in the virtual table at negative offsets relative to the vtable pointer. Accessing virtual base members requires an extra level of indirection through this offset, incurring a small runtime performance penalty.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A classic C++ standard library example:
`std::ios` is the virtual base class.
`std::istream` inherits `virtual public std::ios`.
`std::ostream` inherits `virtual public std::ios`.
`std::iostream` inherits from both `std::istream` and `std::ostream`.
Because of virtual inheritance, `std::iostream` has only ONE stream buffer and format state, not two!
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class PoweredDevice {
public:
    int wattage = 150;
    PoweredDevice() { std::cout << "PoweredDevice constructor\n"; }
};

// Virtual inheritance ensures only ONE PoweredDevice subobject
class Scanner : virtual public PoweredDevice {
public:
    Scanner() { std::cout << "Scanner constructor\n"; }
};

class Printer : virtual public PoweredDevice {
public:
    Printer() { std::cout << "Printer constructor\n"; }
};

// Hybrid inheritance
class AllInOne : public Scanner, public Printer {
public:
    AllInOne() { std::cout << "AllInOne constructor\n"; }
};

int main() {
    AllInOne machine;
    // Without virtual inheritance, machine.wattage would be AMBIGUOUS!
    std::cout << "Wattage: " << machine.wattage << "W\n"; // Unambiguous!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class Person {
protected:
    std::string name;
public:
    explicit Person(const std::string& n) : name(n) {
        std::cout << "Person initialized: " << name << "\n";
    }
};

class Student : virtual public Person {
protected:
    int studentID;
public:
    Student(const std::string& n, int id) : Person(n), studentID(id) {}
};

class Faculty : virtual public Person {
protected:
    std::string department;
public:
    Faculty(const std::string& n, const std::string& dept) : Person(n), department(dept) {}
};

// Most-derived class must initialize virtual base Person directly!
class TeachingAssistant : public Student, public Faculty {
public:
    TeachingAssistant(const std::string& n, int id, const std::string& dept)
        : Person(n), Student(n, id), Faculty(n, dept) {
        std::cout << "TA created: " << name << ", ID: " << studentID 
                  << ", Dept: " << department << "\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    TeachingAssistant ta("Kiran", 9021, "Computer Science");
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

struct A {
    int val = 42;
    A() { std::cout << "A constructed\n"; }
    virtual ~A() = default;
};

struct B : virtual A {
    B() { std::cout << "B constructed\n"; }
};

struct C : virtual A {
    C() { std::cout << "C constructed\n"; }
};

struct D : B, C {
    D() { std::cout << "D constructed\n"; }
};

int main() {
    D d;
    std::cout << "Accessing d.val: " << d.val << "\n";
    
    // Pointer verification
    A* a_via_b = static_cast<B*>(&d);
    A* a_via_c = static_cast<C*>(&d);
    
    std::cout << "A via B address: " << (void*)a_via_b << "\n";
    std::cout << "A via C address: " << (void*)a_via_c << "\n";
    std::cout << "Are both A pointers identical? " 
              << (a_via_b == a_via_c ? "YES (Single subobject)" : "NO") << "\n";
    return 0;
}

The medium example shows TeachingAssistant initializing the virtual base Person directly in its constructor. The advanced example explicitly checks pointer addresses to verify that both paths point to the exact same shared A subobject.

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_INHERITANCE_ACCESS_MODES_001
CATEGORY: Inheritance
SUBCATEGORY: C++ Inheritance Access Modes
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Comparison and Deep Dive
QUESTION: What are the C++ inheritance access modes (public, protected, and private inheritance), and how do they affect inherited members?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is private inheritance?
How does public inheritance differ from private inheritance?
Explain public protected and private inheritance in C++
Difference between public and private inheritance
C++ inheritance access specifiers table
When to use private inheritance?
Protected inheritance in C++
Inheritance accessibility rules
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
public inheritance
private inheritance
protected inheritance
inheritance access modes
is-a vs implemented in terms of
access specifier table
accessibility matrix
derived class access
empty base optimization
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
C++ supports three inheritance access modes: public, protected, and private. Public inheritance preserves base member accessibility (modeling 'is-a'); protected inheritance makes inherited public and protected members protected in the derived class; private inheritance makes all inherited public and protected members private (modeling 'implemented-in-terms-of'). In all three modes, private members of the base class remain completely inaccessible to the derived class.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
When deriving a class in C++, the access specifier in the class header determines the maximum accessibility of inherited members in the derived class:
1. `class D : public B`: Public members of `B` remain `public` in `D`; protected members of `B` remain `protected` in `D`. This models an IS-A relationship and allows implicit upcasting (`B* ptr = new D()`).
2. `class D : protected B`: Both public and protected members of `B` become `protected` in `D`. Outside code cannot access them, but subsequent derived classes can.
3. `class D : private B`: Both public and protected members of `B` become `private` in `D`. This models an 'implemented-in-terms-of' relationship. Outside code and further derived classes cannot access inherited members, and implicit upcasting is disabled for external code.
Base class `private` members are NEVER accessible in `D` under any inheritance mode.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Inheritance access modes in C++ control visibility and polymorphism:

1. The Accessibility Matrix:
| Base Member Access | Inherited via `public` | Inherited via `protected` | Inherited via `private` |
|:-------------------|:----------------------|:--------------------------|:------------------------|
| `public`           | `public` in Derived   | `protected` in Derived    | `private` in Derived    |
| `protected`        | `protected` in Derived| `protected` in Derived    | `private` in Derived    |
| `private`          | Inaccessible in Derived| Inaccessible in Derived  | Inaccessible in Derived |

2. Public Inheritance (`class D : public B`):
- Semantic Meaning: Represents subtyping and behavioral compatibility (IS-A).
- Polymorphism: Derived pointers implicitly convert to base pointers anywhere in code.
- Rule: Follows Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP).

3. Private Inheritance (`class D : private B`):
- Semantic Meaning: Represents composition/implementation reuse ('implemented-in-terms-of').
- Polymorphism: Upcasting to `B*` is only permitted inside member functions and friends of `D`. External code cannot cast `D*` to `B*`.
- When to use: When `D` needs access to protected members of `B`, or needs to override virtual functions of `B`, but `D` is NOT a `B`. Also used for the Empty Base Optimization (EBO).
- Alternative: Composition (`D` contains a member variable of type `B`). General guideline: Prefer composition over private inheritance unless overriding virtual functions or utilizing EBO.

4. Protected Inheritance (`class D : protected B`):
- Rarely used. Restricts outside usage while allowing further subclasses (`class E : public D`) to access `B`'s public and protected interfaces as protected members.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine borrowing your big brother's toy box!
- Public inheritance: You leave the toy box open on the porch so all your neighborhood friends can play with the toys too.
- Protected inheritance: You take the toy box inside your house so only you and your little sisters can play with it.
- Private inheritance: You hide the toy box inside your personal secret closet. Nobody outside your bedroom can touch it!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
In C++:
`class Derived : public Base` -> Everyone outside can still use Base's public methods.
`class Derived : private Base` -> Base's methods are locked inside Derived. The outside world cannot call them!
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
Think of security levels:
Public inheritance is like having an open school library.
Protected inheritance is like a study room only for registered students.
Private inheritance is like the principal's private locker.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Remember default inheritance access:
`class D : B` defaults to `private` inheritance!
`struct D : B` defaults to `public` inheritance!
Always write `public` explicitly when creating class hierarchies.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Why use private inheritance instead of composition?
1. When you need to override a virtual function in the base class.
2. When you need access to protected members of the base class.
3. For Empty Base Optimization (EBO) to achieve zero size overhead.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Restoring Access: You can selectively restore access of private/protected inherited members using `using`:
```cpp
class Derived : private Base {
public:
    using Base::specificMethod; // Promotes specificMethod back to public!
};
```
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
In standard library policy-based designs (such as `std::tuple` or allocator implementations), private inheritance is leveraged for Empty Base Optimization (EBO). If a base class has no non-static data members, C++ allows the derived class to occupy 0 bytes for that base subobject when inherited, whereas a member variable must occupy at least 1 byte.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A `Stack` implemented using a `std::vector`:
If `Stack` publicly inherited from `std::vector`, users could call `stack.insert(middle)` or `stack.clear()`, violating the LIFO contract!
By using **private inheritance** (`class Stack : private std::vector<int>`), `Stack` reuses the vector's memory storage internally, but completely hides vector methods from the outside world, exposing only `push()`, `pop()`, and `top()`.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Base {
public:
    void publicMethod() const { std::cout << "Base public method\n"; }
protected:
    void protectedMethod() const { std::cout << "Base protected method\n"; }
private:
    void privateMethod() const { std::cout << "Base private method\n"; }
};

class PublicDerived : public Base {
public:
    void test() {
        publicMethod();    // OK: public in Base -> public in Derived
        protectedMethod(); // OK: protected in Base -> protected in Derived
        // privateMethod(); // ERROR: private in Base is inaccessible!
    }
};

class PrivateDerived : private Base {
public:
    void test() {
        publicMethod();    // OK inside Derived: becomes private in Derived
        protectedMethod(); // OK inside Derived: becomes private in Derived
    }
};

int main() {
    PublicDerived pub;
    pub.publicMethod(); // OK: callable from outside

    PrivateDerived priv;
    // priv.publicMethod(); // COMPILER ERROR: publicMethod is private in PrivateDerived!
    priv.test(); // OK: calls internally
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>

// Modeling Stack via Private Inheritance to prevent illegal vector operations
class IntStack : private std::vector<int> {
public:
    void push(int val) {
        this->push_back(val); // Allowed inside IntStack
    }

    void pop() {
        if (!this->empty()) {
            this->pop_back();
        }
    }

    int top() const {
        return this->back();
    }

    bool isEmpty() const {
        return this->empty();
    }

    size_t size() const {
        return std::vector<int>::size(); // Expose only size, not random access
    }
};

int main() {
    IntStack stack;
    stack.push(10);
    stack.push(20);
    stack.push(30);

    std::cout << "Top element: " << stack.top() << "\n";
    stack.pop();
    std::cout << "Top after pop: " << stack.top() << "\n";

    // stack.push_back(99); // COMPILER ERROR: push_back is private!
    // stack[0] = 5;        // COMPILER ERROR: operator[] is private!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Engine {
public:
    virtual void start() { std::cout << "Engine started\n"; }
    virtual ~Engine() = default;
};

// Public Inheritance: Car IS-A Engine (Bad design conceptually, but demonstrates upcasting)
class PublicCar : public Engine {};

// Private Inheritance: Car IS IMPLEMENTED IN TERMS OF Engine
class PrivateCar : private Engine {
public:
    void drive() {
        start(); // OK: Engine::start called internally
        std::cout << "Car is moving\n";
    }
};

void testEngine(Engine* e) {
    e->start();
}

int main() {
    PublicCar pubCar;
    testEngine(&pubCar); // OK: Implicit upcast permitted for public inheritance

    PrivateCar privCar;
    privCar.drive(); // OK: public interface of PrivateCar
    // testEngine(&privCar); // COMPILER ERROR: 'Engine' is an inaccessible base of 'PrivateCar'!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example contrasts calling `publicMethod()` on `PublicDerived` vs `PrivateDerived`. The medium example implements an IntStack privately inheriting from `std::vector` to hide vector's arbitrary insertion methods while reusing its storage. The advanced example demonstrates how public inheritance allows external upcasting (`Engine* e = &pubCar;`) while private inheritance forbids it.

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_POLYMORPHISM_RUNTIME_001
CATEGORY: Polymorphism
SUBCATEGORY: Runtime Polymorphism
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python, TypeScript, Swift, Kotlin
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Concept and Implementation
QUESTION: What is runtime polymorphism in C++, how is it achieved, and how does dynamic binding work?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is runtime polymorphism?
Explain runtime polymorphism with an example
How does runtime polymorphism work in C++?
What is dynamic binding and dynamic dispatch?
Late binding vs early binding in C++
Runtime polymorphism syntax and example
Runtime polymorphism kya hota hai
Runtime polymorphism mhanje kay
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
runtime polymorphism
dynamic polymorphism
late binding
dynamic binding
dynamic dispatch
virtual functions
method overriding
base class pointer
vtable
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Runtime polymorphism (also called dynamic polymorphism or late binding) is the ability of an object-oriented program to resolve which overridden method to execute at runtime rather than compile time. In C++, it is achieved using `virtual` functions and invoked via base class pointers or references.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Runtime polymorphism allows a single interface (such as `Shape::draw()`) to trigger different implementations depending on the actual underlying type of the object at runtime. In C++, when a member function is declared `virtual` in the base class and overridden in derived classes, calling that function through a base class pointer (`Shape* s = new Circle(); s->draw();`) resolves dynamically at runtime to `Circle::draw()`. Without the `virtual` keyword, C++ performs compile-time static binding, calling the base class method regardless of the actual object type.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
Runtime polymorphism is the cornerstone of object-oriented design and the Open/Closed Principle.

1. Mechanism in C++:
- Base class declares the function with the `virtual` keyword: `virtual void draw() const;`
- Derived classes override the function: `void draw() const override;`
- Invocations must occur through a **pointer or reference** to the base class: `Base* ptr = &derived; ptr->func();`
- If invoked on a value object directly (`Base b = derived; b.func();`), object slicing occurs, and static binding takes over, defeating runtime polymorphism.

2. Static Binding vs Dynamic Binding:
- Static Binding (Early Binding): The compiler determines the exact memory address of the function call during compilation based on the static type of the variable. Fast, inlinable, but rigid.
- Dynamic Binding (Late Binding): The compiler generates code that queries the object's virtual method table (`vtable`) via its `vptr` at runtime to obtain the function pointer. Slightly slower due to pointer indirection, but highly extensible.

3. Cross-Language Comparisons:
- C++: Opt-in runtime polymorphism via `virtual`. By default, member functions are non-virtual (static binding) for zero-overhead principle.
- Java: Opt-out runtime polymorphism. By default, all non-static, non-private methods are virtual unless explicitly marked `final`.
- Python: Pure dynamic duck-typing; all method lookups resolve at runtime.
- C#: Explicit opt-in using `virtual` in base and `override` in derived classes.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you have a universal game remote controller with a big red 'ACTION' button. If you plug the controller into a toy racing car and press 'ACTION', the car zooms forward! If you plug the exact same controller into a toy helicopter and press 'ACTION', it spins its propellers and flies up! The remote doesn't need to know what toy is plugged in ahead of time—it just sends the command, and the toy knows what to do! That's runtime polymorphism.
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Runtime polymorphism means the decision of which function to run is made while the program is RUNNING, not when it is being compiled.
If you have a Base pointer pointing to a Derived object, calling a `virtual` function runs the Derived version!
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In a school system:
Base class: Person (virtual method `work()`)
Teacher overrides `work()` to teach.
Student overrides `work()` to study.
Janitor overrides `work()` to clean.
If you have a list of Persons, calling `person->work()` does the right job automatically for each one!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Requirements for runtime polymorphism in C++:
1. Inheritance between classes.
2. The function must be declared `virtual` in the base class.
3. Access must be via a Base pointer (`Base*`) or Base reference (`Base&`).
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Always mark overridden functions in derived classes with the `override` keyword (C++11). This instructs the compiler to catch signature mismatches (such as missing `const` or misspelled function names) at compile time.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Polymorphic Base classes must declare a virtual destructor (`virtual ~Base() = default;`). Deleting a derived object via a `Base*` when `Base` lacks a virtual destructor invokes undefined behavior and leaks derived resources.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Performance implications: Virtual function calls prevent inline expansion by default and incur a cache-miss penalty on the vtable pointer dereference. Compilers use devirtualization optimization when the concrete type can be deduced at compile time.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
An audio device system:
`AudioOutput` is the base class with `virtual void outputSound(const Buffer& b);`
Derived classes: `HeadphonesOutput`, `BluetoothSpeakerOutput`, `HDMIOutput`.
When the media player streams music, it simply calls `activeOutput->outputSound(buffer)`. It doesn't need a huge switch-case block checking what device is plugged in!
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <memory>

class Animal {
public:
    virtual void speak() const {
        std::cout << "Animal makes a sound.\n";
    }
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
};

class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    void speak() const override {
        std::cout << "Dog barks: Woof Woof!\n";
    }
};

class Cat : public Animal {
public:
    void speak() const override {
        std::cout << "Cat meows: Meow Meow!\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    // Base pointers pointing to derived objects
    Animal* a1 = new Dog();
    Animal* a2 = new Cat();

    a1->speak(); // Dynamically binds to Dog::speak
    a2->speak(); // Dynamically binds to Cat::speak

    delete a1;
    delete a2;
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <memory>

class PaymentMethod {
public:
    virtual void processPayment(double amount) const = 0;
    virtual ~PaymentMethod() = default;
};

class CreditCard : public PaymentMethod {
public:
    void processPayment(double amount) const override {
        std::cout << "Processing Credit Card payment of $" << amount << " with 2% fee.\n";
    }
};

class PayPal : public PaymentMethod {
public:
    void processPayment(double amount) const override {
        std::cout << "Processing PayPal payment of $" << amount << " instantly.\n";
    }
};

class Crypto : public PaymentMethod {
public:
    void processPayment(double amount) const override {
        std::cout << "Processing Bitcoin payment of $" << amount << " on the blockchain.\n";
    }
};

void checkout(const PaymentMethod& method, double total) {
    method.processPayment(total); // Runtime polymorphism via reference!
}

int main() {
    CreditCard card;
    PayPal paypal;
    Crypto btc;

    checkout(card, 150.0);
    checkout(paypal, 45.0);
    checkout(btc, 1200.0);
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <memory>

class DocumentElement {
public:
    virtual void render() const = 0;
    virtual ~DocumentElement() {
        std::cout << "~DocumentElement destructor\n";
    }
};

class TextBlock : public DocumentElement {
    std::string text;
public:
    explicit TextBlock(std::string t) : text(std::move(t)) {}
    void render() const override {
        std::cout << "[Text: " << text << "]\n";
    }
    ~TextBlock() override {
        std::cout << "~TextBlock destructor\n";
    }
};

class ImageBlock : public DocumentElement {
    std::string url;
public:
    explicit ImageBlock(std::string u) : url(std::move(u)) {}
    void render() const override {
        std::cout << "[Image: " << url << "]\n";
    }
    ~ImageBlock() override {
        std::cout << "~ImageBlock destructor\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<DocumentElement>> page;
    page.push_back(std::make_unique<TextBlock>("Welcome to OOP Master"));
    page.push_back(std::make_unique<ImageBlock>("https://oopmaster.dev/logo.png"));

    std::cout << "--- Rendering Document ---\n";
    for (const auto& elem : page) {
        elem->render(); // Polymorphic dispatch
    }

    std::cout << "--- Cleaning Up Document ---\n";
    page.clear(); // Polymorphic destruction
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example shows base pointer dispatch with Dog and Cat. The medium example demonstrates runtime polymorphism through a C++ reference (`const PaymentMethod&`) in a checkout function. The advanced example manages a polymorphic collection of document elements using smart pointers (`std::unique_ptr`), verifying polymorphic destruction.

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_POLYMORPHISM_VIRTUAL_FUNCS_001
CATEGORY: Polymorphism
SUBCATEGORY: Virtual Functions
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, C#
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Deep Dive
QUESTION: Why do we use virtual functions in C++, and what happens if a function is not declared virtual?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Why do we use virtual functions?
What is a virtual function in C++?
Why virtual functions are needed?
What happens if a member function is not virtual?
Purpose of virtual keyword in C++
Virtual function explanation and syntax
Virtual function override in C++
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
virtual function
virtual keyword
why virtual functions
static binding vs dynamic binding
method overriding
override keyword
late binding
pure virtual function
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
We use virtual functions in C++ to achieve runtime polymorphism (dynamic dispatch). When a function is declared `virtual` in a base class, calling it through a base class pointer or reference executes the derived class's overridden version; if it is not declared virtual, the compiler performs static binding and calls the base class function regardless of the actual object type.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
In C++, member function calls are statically bound at compile time by default to adhere to the zero-overhead principle. Without the `virtual` keyword, the compiler resolves function calls based strictly on the pointer's declared type, not the object's runtime type. Declaring a function `virtual` tells the compiler: "Do not resolve this function call at compile time. Instead, generate instructions to check the object's vtable at runtime and invoke the most-derived implementation." This allows software developers to write extensible, open-ended code that interacts with new derived types without modifying existing base code.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. The Core Problem Without `virtual`:
Suppose you have:
```cpp
class Base {
public:
    void show() { std::cout << "Base\n"; }
};
class Derived : public Base {
public:
    void show() { std::cout << "Derived\n"; }
};
```
If you do:
```cpp
Base* p = new Derived();
p->show(); // PRINTS "Base"!
```
Because `show()` is not virtual, the compiler performs static binding based solely on the type of `p` (`Base*`). The derived class's `show()` is completely ignored.

2. The Solution With `virtual`:
```cpp
class Base {
public:
    virtual void show() { std::cout << "Base\n"; }
};
```
Now:
```cpp
Base* p = new Derived();
p->show(); // PRINTS "Derived"!
```
The compiler inserts a hidden vtable pointer (`vptr`) into the object. At runtime, `p->show()` queries `p->vptr->vtable[0]()`, which points to `Derived::show()`.

3. Why C++ Makes Virtual Functions Opt-In:
Bjarne Stroustrup designed C++ with the zero-overhead principle: "If you don't use it, you don't pay for it." Virtual functions add:
- Memory overhead: One pointer (`vptr`, typically 8 bytes) per object.
- Performance overhead: Indirect function call through a table, inhibiting inlining.
Therefore, only functions intended for polymorphic overriding are declared `virtual`.

4. Rules of Virtual Functions:
- Must be non-static member functions of a class.
- Once a function is declared `virtual` in a base class, it remains `virtual` across ALL derived classes down the inheritance chain, even if the derived class omits the `virtual` keyword.
- Constructors CANNOT be virtual (an object's type must be known before memory is initialized).
- Destructors CAN and SHOULD be virtual in polymorphic base classes.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you have a magic doorbell. If a normal doorbell rings, it always makes a boring 'Ding-Dong' sound no matter who is standing outside. But with a 'virtual' magic doorbell, it looks at who is standing outside: if Santa Claus is outside, it plays Christmas bells! If a firefighter is outside, it plays a siren! The word 'virtual' is the magic that lets the door look at who is really there!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Why use `virtual`?
Without `virtual`: Base pointer calls Base function.
With `virtual`: Base pointer calls Derived function.
It lets you treat different derived objects through a common base pointer and have each one do its own unique action!
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
If you write a video game:
`virtual void attack()` in class `Character`.
Warrior attacks with a sword.
Archer attacks with a bow.
Mage attacks with a fireball.
Using `character->attack()` runs the right attack automatically!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Syntax:
```cpp
class Base {
public:
    virtual void func(); // declared virtual
};
class Derived : public Base {
public:
    void func() override; // override in derived
};
```
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Always use the `override` specifier in derived classes. If you accidentally write `void func(int)` instead of `void func()`, `override` causes a compilation error instead of quietly creating a new overload.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Virtual functions can have default parameter values, but default parameters are statically bound at compile time based on the pointer type! Never change default argument values in overridden virtual functions.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Covariant Return Types: An overridden virtual function can return a pointer or reference to a derived type rather than the base type declared in the base virtual function: `virtual Base* clone()` in Base can be overridden as `Derived* clone() override` in Derived.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A GUI window system:
`Widget` base class with `virtual void onPaint()`.
`Button`, `TextBox`, and `Slider` inherit from `Widget`.
When the operating system asks a window to refresh its screen, it iterates through all widgets:
`for (Widget* w : widgets) w->onPaint();`
Each button, textbox, and slider paints itself with its custom graphics without the window manager knowing anything about their specific types.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class WithoutVirtual {
public:
    void print() const { std::cout << "WithoutVirtual::Base\n"; }
};
class DerivedNoVirtual : public WithoutVirtual {
public:
    void print() const { std::cout << "DerivedNoVirtual::Derived\n"; }
};

class WithVirtual {
public:
    virtual void print() const { std::cout << "WithVirtual::Base\n"; }
    virtual ~WithVirtual() = default;
};
class DerivedWithVirtual : public WithVirtual {
public:
    void print() const override { std::cout << "DerivedWithVirtual::Derived\n"; }
};

int main() {
    std::cout << "--- 1. Without Virtual Keyword (Static Binding) ---\n";
    WithoutVirtual* p1 = new DerivedNoVirtual();
    p1->print(); // Calls Base::print!

    std::cout << "\n--- 2. With Virtual Keyword (Dynamic Binding) ---\n";
    WithVirtual* p2 = new DerivedWithVirtual();
    p2->print(); // Calls Derived::print!

    delete p1;
    delete p2;
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <memory>

class Hero {
public:
    virtual void castUltimate() const {
        std::cout << "Hero unleashes basic energy burst!\n";
    }
    virtual ~Hero() = default;
};

class Mage : public Hero {
public:
    void castUltimate() const override {
        std::cout << "Mage summons a catastrophic Meteor Storm! 🔥\n";
    }
};

class Paladin : public Hero {
public:
    void castUltimate() const override {
        std::cout << "Paladin casts Divine Invulnerability Shield! 🛡️\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Hero>> party;
    party.push_back(std::make_unique<Mage>());
    party.push_back(std::make_unique<Paladin>());

    for (const auto& hero : party) {
        hero->castUltimate(); // Virtual function dispatches to Mage then Paladin!
    }
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Base {
public:
    // Warning: Default argument is statically bound!
    virtual void show(int x = 10) {
        std::cout << "Base::show, x = " << x << "\n";
    }
    virtual ~Base() = default;
};

class Derived : public Base {
public:
    // Even if Derived specifies a different default, Base default is used when called via Base*!
    void show(int x = 99) override {
        std::cout << "Derived::show, x = " << x << "\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    Base* p = new Derived();
    p->show(); // Executes Derived::show, but with Base's default argument (x = 10)!
    delete p;
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example directly proves the difference: without `virtual`, `Base::print` is called; with `virtual`, `Derived::print` is executed. The medium example shows polymorphic dispatch in a gaming party. The advanced example demonstrates a famous C++ gotcha: virtual dispatch selects the function body dynamically, but default argument values are bound statically!
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<

WithoutVirtual::Base

--- 2. With Virtual Keyword (Dynamic Binding) ---
DerivedWithVirtual::Derived
[medium]

Paladin casts Divine Invulnerability Shield! 🛡️
[advanced]
Derived::show, x = 10
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT
COMMON_MISTAKES<<
Thinking that declaring a function virtual in a derived class makes it polymorphic upwards (it must be virtual in the base class).
Overriding a virtual function with different default arguments, leading to subtle bugs.
Forgetting the virtual destructor in base classes.
Calling virtual functions inside constructors or destructors (virtual dispatch is disabled inside constructors and destructors!).

cannot instantiate
subclass implementation
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
A pure virtual function is a virtual function declared with `= 0` in its prototype (`virtual void func() = 0;`), providing no mandatory implementation in the base class and rendering the class abstract. An abstract class cannot be instantiated directly and serves as a strict interface contract that derived classes must implement.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
A pure virtual function is declared in a base class using `= 0`: `virtual void draw() = 0;`. Any class containing at least one pure virtual function is an **Abstract Class**. You cannot create objects of an abstract class (`Shape s;` results in a compilation error). Abstract classes define a standardized interface and enforce that every non-abstract derived class provides concrete implementations for all pure virtual functions. If a derived class fails to override even one pure virtual function, it also becomes an abstract class and cannot be instantiated.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. Syntax and Definition:
`virtual ReturnType functionName(Parameters) = 0;`
The `= 0` syntax is known as the 'pure-specifier'.

2. Characteristics of Abstract Classes:
- Cannot be instantiated: `AbstractClass obj;` -> COMPILER ERROR: `cannot declare variable 'obj' to be of abstract type`.
- Pointers and References: You CAN declare pointers and references to an abstract class (`AbstractClass* ptr = new ConcreteDerived();`). This is the primary way runtime polymorphism is consumed.
- Constructors: Abstract classes CAN have constructors! They execute when a derived class object is created to initialize base class members.
- Destructor: Must declare a `virtual ~AbstractClass() = default;` to ensure polymorphic deletion.

3. Can Pure Virtual Functions Have a Body in C++?
YES! This is a unique C++ feature. A pure virtual function can have an implementation defined outside the class definition:
```cpp
class Base {
public:
    virtual void log() = 0;
};
void Base::log() { std::cout << "Default log implementation\n"; }
```
Derived classes must still override `log()`, but can explicitly call `Base::log()` inside their implementation.

4. Pure Virtual Destructors:
A class can be made abstract by declaring its destructor pure virtual:
```cpp
class AbstractHelper {
public:
    virtual ~AbstractHelper() = 0;
};
AbstractHelper::~AbstractHelper() {} // MUST provide body, as derived destructors call it!
```
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine a coloring book! The coloring book has a picture of an empty outline labeled 'Animal'. You cannot pet the outline because it is just an idea (an abstract class). But the book tells you: 'You MUST draw a real animal here!' You can draw a Dog and color it in, or draw a Cat and color it in. The outline enforces the rule that an animal must be drawn!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
A pure virtual function has `= 0` at the end:
`virtual void play() = 0;`
This tells C++: "The base class doesn't know how to play. Every child class MUST write its own `play()` code, or the code won't compile!"
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In an exam grading program:
`virtual double calculateGrade() = 0;` in base class `Exam`.
MathExam, HistoryExam, and ScienceExam all have completely different grading formulas. The base class enforces that each exam must write its own formula.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Difference between virtual and pure virtual:
- Virtual: Provides a default body. Derived classes CAN override it, but don't have to.
- Pure Virtual: Has `= 0`. Derived classes MUST override it to be instantiable.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Abstract classes can have member variables, non-virtual methods, and regular virtual methods alongside pure virtual functions.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
In C++, an abstract class with only pure virtual functions and no data members is equivalent to an `interface` in Java and C#.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
In the vtable, a pure virtual function that is not overridden is typically populated with a pointer to a runtime error handler function (such as `__cxa_pure_virtual`), which aborts the program with a 'pure virtual function call' error if invoked illegally during construction/destruction.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A Database Connector library:
`DatabaseConnection` is an abstract class with:
- `virtual void connect(string host) = 0;`
- `virtual ResultSet executeQuery(string query) = 0;`
- `virtual void disconnect() = 0;`
Concrete derived classes: `PostgreSQLConnection`, `MySQLConnection`, `SQLiteConnection`.
Application code interacts only with `DatabaseConnection*`, completely decoupled from specific database vendor drivers.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

// Abstract Class
class Shape {
public:
    virtual void draw() const = 0; // Pure Virtual Function
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
};

class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    void draw() const override {
        std::cout << "Drawing a Circle ⭕\n";
    }
};

class Square : public Shape {
public:
    void draw() const override {
        std::cout << "Drawing a Square ⬛\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    // Shape s; // COMPILER ERROR: cannot instantiate abstract class!
    Shape* s1 = new Circle();
    Shape* s2 = new Square();

    s1->draw();
    s2->draw();

    delete s1;
    delete s2;
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

// Abstract Base Class with Constructor & Partial Implementation
class NotificationService {
protected:
    std::string senderName;
public:
    explicit NotificationService(std::string name) : senderName(std::move(name)) {}

    // Concrete non-virtual method
    void logDispatch(const std::string& recipient) const {
        std::cout << "[" << senderName << "] Dispatched to: " << recipient << "\n";
    }

    // Pure virtual method
    virtual void sendMessage(const std::string& recipient, const std::string& message) = 0;

    virtual ~NotificationService() = default;
};

class EmailNotification : public NotificationService {
public:
    EmailNotification() : NotificationService("EmailService") {}

    void sendMessage(const std::string& recipient, const std::string& message) override {
        std::cout << "Sending Email to <" << recipient << ">: " << message << "\n";
        logDispatch(recipient);
    }
};

class SMSNotification : public NotificationService {
public:
    SMSNotification() : NotificationService("SMSService") {}

    void sendMessage(const std::string& recipient, const std::string& message) override {
        std::cout << "Sending SMS to +" << recipient << ": " << message << "\n";
        logDispatch(recipient);
    }
};

int main() {
    EmailNotification email;
    SMSNotification sms;

    email.sendMessage("user@example.com", "Your OTP is 9876");
    sms.sendMessage("15551234567", "Your verification code is 4321");
    return 0;
}

#include <iostream>

// Demonstrating pure virtual function WITH A BODY in C++
class ProtocolHandler {
public:
    virtual void authenticate() = 0; // Pure virtual
    virtual ~ProtocolHandler() = default;
};

// Providing a body for pure virtual function
void ProtocolHandler::authenticate() {
    std::cout << "Default SSL handshake and credential verification.\n";
}

class CustomProtocol : public ProtocolHandler {
public:
    void authenticate() override {
        // Derived class must override, but can explicitly delegate to base implementation!
        ProtocolHandler::authenticate();
        std::cout << "Custom token validation complete.\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    CustomProtocol proto;
    proto.authenticate();
    return 0;
}

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_POLYMORPHISM_VTABLES_001
CATEGORY: Polymorphism
SUBCATEGORY: Vtables and Dynamic Dispatch
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Internal Implementation Mechanism
QUESTION: What is a vtable (Virtual Method Table) and vptr in C++, and how does the compiler implement runtime polymorphism under the hood?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Explain vtables like I'm five
What is a vtable?
What is a vptr in C++?
How do vtables work in C++?
Virtual table internal mechanism
How compiler implements virtual functions
Vtable memory layout and overhead
Dynamic dispatch mechanism in C++
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
vtable
vptr
virtual method table
virtual table pointer
dynamic dispatch
late binding
compiler implementation
internal mechanism
function pointers array
Itanium ABI
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
A vtable (virtual method table) is a compiler-generated static lookup table of function pointers created for every class that declares or inherits virtual functions. Each object of such a class contains an invisible pointer member called `vptr` (virtual table pointer) that points to its class's vtable, allowing virtual function calls to be resolved dynamically at runtime through pointer dereferencing.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
When a class defines a virtual function, the C++ compiler constructs a single static table (`vtable`) for that class containing pointers to all its virtual member functions. Every object instantiated from that class receives an invisible pointer, the `vptr`, typically stored as the very first 8 bytes of the object. When code calls `ptr->virtualFunction()`, the compiler does not call a hardcoded address; instead, it generates machine code that performs three steps:
1. Dereferences `ptr` to access the object's `vptr`.
2. Indexes into the `vtable` at the function's assigned slot offset (e.g., slot 0).
3. Invokes the function pointer stored at that slot (`call *vtable[slot]`).
This three-step indirection is dynamic dispatch.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. The Compiler-Generated Components:
For every polymorphic class:
- `vtable` (Virtual Table): An array of function pointers stored in the read-only data segment (`.rodata`) of the executable. There is exactly ONE vtable per class, shared by all instances of that class.
- `vptr` (Virtual Table Pointer): A hidden pointer added to the object layout. Every instance of a polymorphic class has its own `vptr`.

2. Memory Layout:
Consider:
```cpp
class Base {
    int a;
    virtual void f();
    virtual void g();
};
```
In a 64-bit architecture:
- Byte 0-7: `vptr` (pointing to `Base::vtable`)
- Byte 8-11: `int a`
- Byte 12-15: Padding for alignment
- Total `sizeof(Base)` = 16 bytes! (Without virtual functions, `sizeof(Base)` would be only 4 bytes).

3. The Dispatch Step-by-Step:
When executing:
```cpp
Base* p = new Derived();
p->g();
```
The compiler translates `p->g()` into assembly equivalent to:
```c
// Pseudo-C translation:
typedef void (*FuncPtr)(Base*);
FuncPtr fn = p->__vptr[1]; // slot 1 for g()
fn(p); // Call with 'this' pointer as hidden first argument
```

4. Vtable Population During Inheritance:
- When `Derived` overrides `f()` but does NOT override `g()`:
  - `Derived::vtable[0]` = address of `Derived::f`
  - `Derived::vtable[1]` = address of `Base::g` (inherited directly)
- If `Derived` adds a new virtual function `h()`, it gets slot 2:
  - `Derived::vtable[2]` = address of `Derived::h`

5. Cost of Virtual Functions:
- Space overhead: +8 bytes per object for `vptr`, plus small static table per class.
- Time overhead: Two pointer dereferences (`p -> vptr -> vtable[idx]`) before the jump.
- Inlining prevention: Compilers cannot inline function calls resolved through function pointers, inhibiting optimizations like loop vectorization.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you go to a big food court where every table has a menu on it. When you press the 'Burger' button on Table 1 (the American table), the waiter checks Table 1's menu and brings you a Cheeseburger. When you press the exact same 'Burger' button on Table 2 (the Indian table), the waiter checks Table 2's menu and brings you a Spicy Veggie Burger! The table's menu is the 'vtable', and the little card on the table is the 'vptr'. Same button, but the menu tells the waiter which kitchen to run to!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
A vtable is simply a list of function addresses that the compiler makes behind the scenes.
When you call a virtual function on a pointer, C++ looks up the list to see which function belongs to the real object.
`vptr` is the pointer inside your object that points to that list.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
Think of speed dial on a smartphone. Button 1 is labeled 'Mom'.
If you hold your friend's phone and press Button 1, it calls their mom.
If you hold your own phone and press Button 1, it calls your mom.
Each phone has its own speed-dial list (vtable). Button 1 is the virtual function!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Notice `sizeof(Class)`: Adding even one virtual function to a class increases its `sizeof` by the size of a pointer (8 bytes on 64-bit systems) because of `vptr`.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
During constructor execution, an object's `vptr` is set to point to the base class vtable while the base constructor runs, and only updated to the derived vtable when the derived constructor begins!
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
That is why virtual function calls inside constructors do NOT dispatch to derived classes: the derived object has not been constructed yet, so the vptr still points to the base vtable.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Under the Itanium C++ ABI, the vtable also contains RTTI (Run-Time Type Information) pointers at offset -8 (used by `typeid` and `dynamic_cast`), and virtual base class offsets at negative offsets for virtual inheritance.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
Operating System Device Drivers:
The OS kernel defines a `BlockDevice` interface. The `vtable` acts like a function pointer dispatch table:
- Slot 0: `readBlock()`
- Slot 1: `writeBlock()`
- Slot 2: `flush()`
When the filesystem needs to save data, it calls `dev->writeBlock(...)`, which jumps through the vtable directly into the NVMe driver or SATA driver.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class BaseWithoutVirtual {
    int data;
};

class BaseWithVirtual {
    int data;
    virtual void func() {}
};

int main() {
    std::cout << "Size of Base WITHOUT virtual: " << sizeof(BaseWithoutVirtual) << " bytes\n";
    std::cout << "Size of Base WITH virtual:    " << sizeof(BaseWithVirtual) << " bytes\n";
    std::cout << "Notice the 8-byte vptr added by the compiler!\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Shape {
public:
    virtual void draw() { std::cout << "Shape::draw (vtable slot 0)\n"; }
    virtual void resize() { std::cout << "Shape::resize (vtable slot 1)\n"; }
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
};

class Circle : public Shape {
public:
    void draw() override { std::cout << "Circle::draw (overrides slot 0)\n"; }
    // resize() is not overridden, so Circle's vtable slot 1 still points to Shape::resize!
};

int main() {
    Shape* s1 = new Shape();
    Shape* s2 = new Circle();

    s1->draw();   // calls Shape::draw
    s1->resize(); // calls Shape::resize

    s2->draw();   // calls Circle::draw (overridden in Circle's vtable)
    s2->resize(); // calls Shape::resize (inherited in Circle's vtable)

    delete s1;
    delete s2;
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Animal {
public:
    Animal() {
        std::cout << "Animal constructor: calling speak()...\n";
        speak(); // Will call Animal::speak, NOT Dog::speak!
    }
    virtual void speak() const {
        std::cout << "Animal sound (Animal vtable active)\n";
    }
    virtual ~Animal() = default;
};

class Dog : public Animal {
public:
    Dog() {
        std::cout << "Dog constructor: calling speak()...\n";
        speak(); // Now calls Dog::speak!
    }
    void speak() const override {
        std::cout << "Dog bark: Woof! (Dog vtable active)\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    std::cout << "--- Constructing Dog ---\n";
    Dog d; // Demonstrates vptr transitions during construction!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example proves the existence of `vptr` by measuring `sizeof`: adding `virtual` expands the class by 8 bytes. The medium example shows how `Circle` overrides slot 0 (`draw()`) while inheriting slot 1 (`resize()`). The advanced example demonstrates how `vptr` points to the `Animal` vtable during `Animal()` execution and only updates to the `Dog` vtable when `Dog()` begins.

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_POLYMORPHISM_COMPILE_TIME_001
CATEGORY: Polymorphism
SUBCATEGORY: Compile-Time Polymorphism
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python (via singledispatch)
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Comparison and Implementation
QUESTION: What is compile-time polymorphism in C++, how is it achieved through function and operator overloading, and how does it compare to runtime polymorphism?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is compile-time polymorphism?
Compile-time polymorphism vs runtime polymorphism
Explain function overloading in C++
Explain operator overloading in C++
Static polymorphism vs dynamic polymorphism in C++
Early binding vs late binding
Static dispatch in C++
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
compile time polymorphism
static polymorphism
early binding
static dispatch
function overloading
operator overloading
name mangling
function signature
no vtable overhead
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Compile-time polymorphism (static polymorphism or early binding) is an OOP mechanism where the compiler selects and binds the appropriate function or operator to execute at compile time based on parameter types and counts. In C++, it is achieved via function overloading, operator overloading, and templates with zero runtime overhead.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Compile-time polymorphism resolves function calls during compilation rather than at runtime. It takes two primary forms in non-template C++:
1. Function Overloading: Multiple functions sharing the exact same name within the same scope but possessing different parameter types, numbers, or const qualifiers (e.g., `print(int)` vs `print(string)`).
2. Operator Overloading: Providing custom implementations for standard C++ operators (like `+`, `==`, `<<`) for user-defined classes (e.g., `Vector2D operator+(const Vector2D&)`).
Because the compiler knows the exact types at compile time, it performs direct function calls with no `vptr` or `vtable` overhead, enabling aggressive compiler optimizations and inlining.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. Function Overloading Mechanism:
- The compiler differentiates overloaded functions using **Name Mangling** (decoration).
- For example, `void add(int, int)` becomes `_Z3addii`, while `void add(double, double)` becomes `_Z3adddd`.
- Function return types do NOT participate in overload resolution: declaring `int calculate()` and `double calculate()` in the same scope is a compile-time error.

2. Operator Overloading:
- Allows user-defined types to behave with the syntactic naturalness of built-in primitives.
- Syntax: `ReturnType operator Symbol (Parameters) { ... }`
- Rules:
  - Cannot create new operators (e.g., cannot invent `operator**`).
  - Cannot alter operator precedence or associativity.
  - Operators that CANNOT be overloaded: `.`, `.*`, `::`, `?:`, `sizeof`, `typeid`.
  - Stream operators (`<<` and `>>`) must be overloaded as free functions (often friends) because the left-hand operand is a stream (`std::ostream&`), not the user class.

3. Comparison with Runtime Polymorphism:
| Feature | Compile-Time Polymorphism | Runtime Polymorphism |
|:--------|:--------------------------|:---------------------|
| Resolution Time | During Compilation | During Execution |
| Mechanism | Overloading, Templates | Virtual functions, Vtables |
| Performance | Zero overhead, Inlinable | Pointer dereference, No inlining |
| Memory Overhead | 0 bytes per object | 8 bytes per object (`vptr`) |
| Flexibility | Fixed at compile time | Extensible with new derived types |
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Think of the word 'LIGHT'! If you say 'Turn on the light', people know you mean a lamp. If you say 'This box is light', people know you mean it is not heavy! You used the exact same word, but based on the other words around it, everyone immediately understands which meaning you intended without having to wait!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Compile-time polymorphism means the compiler already knows which function to call before your program even starts running.
`add(2, 3)` calls the integer version.
`add(2.5, 3.5)` calls the float version.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In math: Plus `+` adds numbers (`5 + 3 = 8`). In strings, `+` concatenates words (`"Hello " + "World"`). That is operator overloading!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Remember: You cannot overload functions based on return type alone! The parameter list MUST be different.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Stream extraction (`<<`) must be implemented as a non-member function:
`std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& os, const MyClass& obj);`
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Template metaprogramming and the Curiously Recurring Template Pattern (CRTP) represent static polymorphism taken to an advanced level, achieving polymorphic interfaces without virtual functions.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Overload resolution follows strict ranking rules in C++: exact match > promotions (e.g., `char` to `int`) > standard conversions (e.g., `int` to `double`) > user-defined conversions > ellipsis (`...`).
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A 2D/3D Vector math library in a game engine:
`Vector2D a(3, 4); Vector2D b(1, 2);`
Using operator overloading, you can simply write `Vector2D c = a + b;` instead of `Vector2D c = a.addVector(b);`, making physics formulas clear and readable.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class Calculator {
public:
    // Function Overloading: same name, different signatures
    int add(int a, int b) {
        return a + b;
    }

    double add(double a, double b) {
        return a + b;
    }

    std::string add(const std::string& a, const std::string& b) {
        return a + b;
    }
};

int main() {
    Calculator calc;
    std::cout << "Integer addition: " << calc.add(10, 20) << "\n";
    std::cout << "Double addition:  " << calc.add(5.5, 4.3) << "\n";
    std::cout << "String addition:  " << calc.add("OOP ", "Master") << "\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Complex {
    double real;
    double imag;
public:
    Complex(double r = 0.0, double i = 0.0) : real(r), imag(i) {}

    // Operator Overloading for '+'
    Complex operator+(const Complex& other) const {
        return Complex(real + other.real, imag + other.imag);
    }

    // Operator Overloading for '=='
    bool operator==(const Complex& other) const {
        return (real == other.real) && (imag == other.imag);
    }

    // Friend stream insertion operator
    friend std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& os, const Complex& c) {
        os << c.real << " + " << c.imag << "i";
        return os;
    }
};

int main() {
    Complex c1(3.0, 4.0);
    Complex c2(1.5, 2.5);

    Complex c3 = c1 + c2; // Calls operator+
    std::cout << "c1 = " << c1 << "\n";
    std::cout << "c2 = " << c2 << "\n";
    std::cout << "c1 + c2 = " << c3 << "\n";
    std::cout << "Are c1 and c2 equal? " << (c1 == c2 ? "Yes" : "No") << "\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

// Prefix and Postfix increment operator overloading
class Counter {
    int count;
public:
    explicit Counter(int c = 0) : count(c) {}

    // Prefix increment: ++c
    Counter& operator++() {
        ++count;
        return *this;
    }

    // Postfix increment: c++ (dummy int parameter distinguishes it!)
    Counter operator++(int) {
        Counter temp = *this; // Save old state
        ++count;
        return temp;          // Return old state
    }

    int getCount() const { return count; }
};

int main() {
    Counter c(5);
    std::cout << "Initial: " << c.getCount() << "\n";

    Counter prefix = ++c; // Increments first, returns new value
    std::cout << "After ++c: c=" << c.getCount() << ", result=" << prefix.getCount() << "\n";

    Counter postfix = c++; // Increments, but returns old value
    std::cout << "After c++: c=" << c.getCount() << ", result=" << postfix.getCount() << "\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example shows function overloading with int, double, and string arguments. The medium example overloads the addition `+`, equality `==`, and stream insertion `<<` operators for a Complex number class. The advanced example demonstrates the difference between prefix `++c` and postfix `c++` operator overloading using the dummy int parameter.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
Integer addition: 30
Double addition:  9.8
String addition:  OOP Master
[medium]
c1 = 3 + 4i
c2 = 1.5 + 2.5i
c1 + c2 = 4.5 + 6.5i
Are c1 and c2 equal? No
[advanced]
Initial: 5
After ++c: c=6, result=6
After c++: c=7, result=6
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT
COMMON_MISTAKES<<
Attempting to overload functions differing only in return type.
Overloading operators with unintuitive semantics (e.g., using `+` to subtract).
Forgetting the dummy `int` parameter when overloading the postfix increment operator.
Attempting to overload non-overloadable operators like `.` or `::`.
>>COMMON_MISTAKES
RELATED_TOPICS<<

>>RELATED_TOPICS

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_ACCESS_SPECIFIERS_RULES_001
CATEGORY: Encapsulation
SUBCATEGORY: Access Specifiers and Accessibility Rules
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python (conventions)
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Beginner
QUESTION_TYPE: Deep Dive and Comparison
QUESTION: What are access specifiers (public, private, protected) in C++ and what are their exact accessibility rules?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What are access specifiers?
Explain public private and protected in C++
Difference between public private and protected
Accessibility rules in C++ classes
Access specifiers table and example
Data hiding with access specifiers
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
access specifiers
public
private
protected
accessibility rules
data hiding
encapsulation
information hiding
member access
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Access specifiers in C++ (`public`, `private`, `protected`) define the scope and visibility of class members. `public` members are accessible from anywhere; `private` members are accessible only from within member functions and friends of that class; `protected` members are accessible within the class itself and its derived classes, but hidden from the general outside world.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
C++ access specifiers enforce encapsulation and data hiding by controlling where members can be accessed:
1. `public`: Members are accessible from any function anywhere in the program. This forms the class's public API.
2. `private`: Members can only be accessed by member functions and friend functions/classes of the same class. This hides internal state and implementation details from external tampering.
3. `protected`: Members can be accessed by member functions and friends of the class, AND by member functions of derived classes. External functions cannot access them.
By default, members of a `class` are `private`, while members of a `struct` are `public`.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. Accessibility Matrix (Inside vs Derived vs Outside):
| Access Specifier | Accessible Inside Own Class? | Accessible in Derived Classes? | Accessible Outside by General Code? |
|:-----------------|:-----------------------------|:------------------------------|:------------------------------------|
| `public`         | YES                          | YES                           | YES                                 |
| `protected`      | YES                          | YES                           | NO                                  |
| `private`        | YES                          | NO                            | NO                                  |

2. Best Practices for Object-Oriented Design:
- Member Variables: Almost always declare data members `private`. Expose access through getters/setters with validation if needed.
- `protected` Variables: Use sparingly. While convenient for derived classes, protected variables create tight coupling between base and derived classes. Changing a protected variable can break all derived classes.
- Public Methods: Keep the public interface minimal, cohesive, and invariant-preserving.

3. Class vs Struct Default Access:
- In C++, a `class` defaults to `private` member access and `private` inheritance.
- A `struct` defaults to `public` member access and `public` inheritance.
- There are no other technical differences between `class` and `struct` in C++.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine your backpack!
- Public: The water bottle in the outside mesh pocket. Anyone can see it and hand it to you!
- Protected: Your school notebook inside your bag. Only you and your teacher can open it and write in it.
- Private: Your personal diary inside a locked secret pocket. Only YOU have the key—nobody else is allowed to peek!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Access specifiers are security gates:
`public`: Open door for everyone.
`private`: Locked door, only the owner has the key.
`protected`: Family-only door (the class and its child classes).
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In a Student class:
`name` is public (everyone can call your name).
`grades` are protected (only teachers and students can see them).
`password` is private (only the student login system can access it).
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Remember: private variables in a base class ARE inherited in memory, but derived class methods simply DO NOT have permission to access their names directly.
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Provide `protected` helper methods rather than `protected` member variables to avoid breaking encapsulation across class hierarchies.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Friend declarations (`friend class`, `friend void func()`) bypass access specifiers entirely, granting full access to private and protected members.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Access control in C++ is a compile-time check, not a runtime security sandbox. Code that uses raw pointer arithmetic or `reinterpret_cast` can technically overwrite private memory, though it produces undefined behavior.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A Bank Account:
- `private`: `double balance;` (Prevents users from directly writing `account.balance = 1000000;`).
- `public`: `deposit(amount)`, `withdraw(amount)`, `getBalance()` (Validates amounts and records audit logs before updating balance).
- `protected`: `calculateInterest()` (Available for SavingsAccount to customize without exposing to regular users).
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class BankAccount {
private:
    double balance = 0.0; // Private: hidden from outside world

protected:
    std::string accountType = "Standard"; // Protected: child classes can read

public:
    void deposit(double amount) { // Public: accessible anywhere
        if (amount > 0) {
            balance += amount;
            std::cout << "Deposited $" << amount << ". New balance: $" << balance << "\n";
        }
    }

    double getBalance() const {
        return balance;
    }
};

int main() {
    BankAccount acc;
    acc.deposit(500.0);
    std::cout << "Current Balance: $" << acc.getBalance() << "\n";

    // acc.balance = 1000000; // COMPILER ERROR: 'balance' is private!
    // acc.accountType;       // COMPILER ERROR: 'accountType' is protected!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class Vehicle {
private:
    std::string secretVIN; // Inaccessible to derived classes
protected:
    int speedKmh = 0;      // Accessible to Vehicle and its derived classes
public:
    explicit Vehicle(std::string vin) : secretVIN(std::move(vin)) {}

    void accelerate(int delta) {
        if (delta > 0) speedKmh += delta;
    }

    int getSpeed() const { return speedKmh; }
};

class SportsCar : public Vehicle {
public:
    explicit SportsCar(std::string vin) : Vehicle(std::move(vin)) {}

    void engageTurbo() {
        speedKmh += 50; // OK: speedKmh is protected in Vehicle!
        std::cout << "Turbo engaged! Speed boosted to " << speedKmh << " km/h.\n";
        // std::cout << secretVIN; // ERROR: secretVIN is private to Vehicle!
    }
};

int main() {
    SportsCar car("VIN12345XYZ");
    car.accelerate(80);
    std::cout << "Cruising at: " << car.getSpeed() << " km/h\n";
    car.engageTurbo();
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

// Proving that struct defaults to public and class defaults to private
struct StructExample {
    int x = 10; // Public by default
};

class ClassExample {
    int y = 20; // Private by default
public:
    int getY() const { return y; }
};

int main() {
    StructExample s;
    std::cout << "Struct default public access: s.x = " << s.x << "\n";

    ClassExample c;
    std::cout << "Class default private access via getter: c.getY() = " << c.getY() << "\n";
    // std::cout << c.y; // COMPILER ERROR: 'int ClassExample::y' is private!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example shows BankAccount protecting balance from outside tampering while providing public deposit and getter methods. The medium example shows SportsCar accessing protected `speedKmh` while being blocked from private `secretVIN`. The advanced example contrasts default access in `struct` vs `class`.

>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT

Assuming protected variables cannot be accessed by other instances of the same class (access is class-based, not instance-based!).
