=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_CPP_FRIEND_MEMBERS_001
CATEGORY: Encapsulation
SUBCATEGORY: Friend Functions and Friend Classes
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Concept and Implementation
QUESTION: What are friend functions and friend classes in C++, and when should they be used?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is a friend function in C++?
What is a friend class in C++?
Explain friend keyword in C++
How to access private members without inheritance?
Friend function syntax and example
Pros and cons of friend functions in C++
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
friend function
friend class
friend keyword
access private members
operator overloading friend
controlled encapsulation bypass
stream insertion operator
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
A friend function or friend class in C++ is an external function or class that is granted full access to the `private` and `protected` members of the class granting friendship using the `friend` keyword. It provides a controlled exception to encapsulation, commonly used for stream operator overloading (`<<`, `>>`) and tightly coupled helper classes.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Encapsulation normally prevents outside functions from accessing private members. However, certain operations—such as overloading stream operators (`std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream&, const MyClass&)`) or collaborating classes (like a `Node` and `LinkedList`)—require direct access to private state. C++ provides the `friend` keyword: by placing `friend void printData(const MyClass&);` inside `MyClass`, `MyClass` explicitly grants `printData` permission to access all its private variables. Friendship is granted by the class itself (you cannot declare yourself a friend of someone else's class), is not mutual, and is not inherited.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. Rules of Friendship in C++:
- Friendship is **granted, not taken**: Only Class `A` can declare who its friends are. External code cannot force friendship.
- Friendship is **not symmetric (not mutual)**: If Class `A` is a friend of Class `B`, Class `B` is NOT automatically a friend of Class `A`.
- Friendship is **not transitive**: If `A` is a friend of `B`, and `B` is a friend of `C`, `A` is NOT a friend of `C`.
- Friendship is **not inherited**: A derived class does not inherit the friends of its base class, nor are the derived class's friends granted access to the base class's private members.

2. Common Use Cases:
- Stream Operator Overloading: `friend std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream&, const MyClass&);`
- Tightly Coupled Data Structures: `class LinkedList` declared as a friend inside `class Node`.
- Unit Testing: Test harnesses declared as friends to inspect private internal state without exposing public getters.

3. Friend Classes:
Declaring `friend class Inspector;` allows every member function of `Inspector` to read and modify all private and protected members of the granting class.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you have a secret diary with a lock on it. Normally, nobody is allowed to read it. But you write on the cover: 'My Best Friend Tim is allowed to open this diary!' Tim can now read your secrets because YOU gave him permission. But Tim cannot let his brother read your diary, and you don't automatically get to read Tim's diary. Friendship must be given by you!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
A friend function is a function outside a class that is given special permission to touch private variables.
You put `friend void myFunc();` inside your class.
Now `myFunc()` can access private variables directly!
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In an exam grading class: `ExamPaper` has private answers. The `Examiner` class is declared as a `friend class` so the examiner can grade the paper directly.
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Syntax:
```cpp
class Box {
    int width;
    friend void printWidth(const Box& b); // Friend function
    friend class BoxInspector;            // Friend class
};
```
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Friend functions are essential for overloading binary operators where the first operand is not your class (like `std::cout << myObj`).
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Friend functions declared inside a class body are also defined inline and discovered via Argument-Dependent Lookup (ADL / Koenig lookup).
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Friendship does not violate encapsulation when used correctly; it actually enhances it. By using friends, you can grant access to tightly coupled collaborators without exposing broad public getters/setters to the entire program.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A Vector and Matrix math library:
`Matrix4x4` needs direct access to the internal float array of `Vector4D` to compute high-speed transformations without function call overhead. `Vector4D` declares `friend class Matrix4x4`.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Box {
private:
    double width = 15.0;

    // Granting friendship to a standalone function
    friend void displayWidth(const Box& b);
};

// Standalone function (NOT a member function of Box)
void displayWidth(const Box& b) {
    // Accessing private member directly!
    std::cout << "Box width accessed by friend function: " << b.width << "\n";
}

int main() {
    Box myBox;
    displayWidth(myBox);
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class Student {
private:
    std::string name;
    int confidentialScore;

public:
    Student(std::string n, int s) : name(std::move(n)), confidentialScore(s) {}

    // Overloading << as a friend function
    friend std::ostream& operator<<(std::ostream& os, const Student& s) {
        os << "Student: " << s.name << " (Internal Score: " << s.confidentialScore << ")";
        return os;
    }

    // Granting friendship to Auditor class
    friend class Auditor;
};

class Auditor {
public:
    void auditStudent(const Student& s) {
        std::cout << "[Auditor Log] Auditing " << s.name 
                  << ": Score verified as " << s.confidentialScore << "\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    Student s1("Aditi", 95);
    std::cout << s1 << "\n"; // Calls friend operator<<

    Auditor auditor;
    auditor.auditStudent(s1); // Calls friend class member
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

// Forward declaration
class LinkedList;

class Node {
private:
    int data;
    Node* next;

    Node(int val) : data(val), next(nullptr) {}

    // Only LinkedList can allocate and manipulate Node internals
    friend class LinkedList;
};

class LinkedList {
    Node* head = nullptr;
public:
    void insert(int val) {
        Node* newNode = new Node(val); // OK: LinkedList is friend of Node
        newNode->next = head;
        head = newNode;
    }

    void print() const {
        Node* curr = head;
        while (curr) {
            std::cout << curr->data << " -> "; // Direct private access
            curr = curr->next;
        }
        std::cout << "nullptr\n";
    }

    ~LinkedList() {
        while (head) {
            Node* temp = head;
            head = head->next;
            delete temp;
        }
    }
};

int main() {
    LinkedList list;
    list.insert(30);
    list.insert(20);
    list.insert(10);
    list.print();
    // Node n(5); // COMPILER ERROR: Node constructor is private!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example shows a standalone friend function accessing a private width variable. The medium example overloads operator<< and demonstrates a friend class Auditor. The advanced example shows a classic idiom: Node's constructor and members are completely private, permitting only LinkedList to create and manage them.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
Box width accessed by friend function: 15
[medium]
Student: Aditi (Internal Score: 95)
[Auditor Log] Auditing Aditi: Score verified as 95
[advanced]
10 -> 20 -> 30 -> nullptr
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT
COMMON_MISTAKES<<
Assuming friendship is reciprocal (`A` is friend of `B` does NOT make `B` friend of `A`).
Assuming friendship is inherited by derived classes.
Overusing friend functions to the point of eliminating class encapsulation.
>>COMMON_MISTAKES
RELATED_TOPICS<<
Access Specifiers
Encapsulation
Operator Overloading
Data Structures Implementation
>>RELATED_TOPICS
FOLLOW_UP_QUESTIONS<<

How does friendship enhance encapsulation rather than weaken it?
Is friendship inherited in C++?
>>FOLLOW_UP_QUESTIONS

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_CPP_STATIC_MEMBERS_001
CATEGORY: Objects and Classes
SUBCATEGORY: Static Members
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Intermediate
QUESTION_TYPE: Concept and Implementation
QUESTION: What are static data members and static member functions in C++, and how do they differ from instance members?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What is a static variable in a class?
What is a static member function?
Explain static keyword in C++ classes
Difference between class variable and instance variable
Why static functions cannot use this pointer
Static members in C++ syntax and example
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
static data member
static member function
class variable vs instance variable
static keyword
no this pointer
shared state across objects
static initialization
ClassName::member
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
Static data members are class-level variables shared by all instances of that class rather than replicated per object. Static member functions are class-level functions that can be called without creating an object (`ClassName::func()`); they operate only on static data and cannot access instance variables or the `this` pointer.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
In C++, regular data members exist independently inside every instantiated object. A **static data member** exists as a single shared variable in the static data segment of memory, shared across all objects of the class. It must be defined outside the class body (in C++11/14) or marked `inline static` (in C++17). A **static member function** belongs to the class itself rather than any instance. Because it has no implicit `this` pointer argument, it can only access other static members and cannot read or write regular instance variables directly.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. Static Data Members:
- Shared State: Stored in global/static memory, not inside individual object instances.
- Size Contribution: Static variables do NOT increase `sizeof(Class)`!
- Out-of-Class Definition: In C++98/C++11, static members must be explicitly defined in a source file:
  `int MyClass::count = 0;`
  In C++17, `inline static int count = 0;` allows direct in-class initialization.

2. Static Member Functions:
- Can be invoked without an object instance: `MyClass::doSomething();`
- No `this` pointer: Cannot call instance methods or access instance variables without an explicit object reference.
- Cannot be declared `virtual`, `const`, or `volatile` (because those specifiers apply to the implicit `this` pointer).

3. Instance vs Static Comparison:
| Property | Instance Member | Static Member |
|:---------|:----------------|:--------------|
| Storage | Inside each object | Once in static memory |
| Lifetime | Object lifetime | Entire program lifetime |
| Access | Via object (`obj.var`) | Via class (`Class::var`) or object |
| `this` pointer | Available | Not available |
| Memory cost | Multiplied per object | Fixed single allocation |
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine a classroom of students! Each student has their own backpack and their own pencil (instance variables). But on the wall, there is ONE big classroom clock that all students share (static variable). If the clock ticks forward, it changes for all students at the exact same time!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Static means shared!
Instance variable: each object gets its own copy.
Static variable: one copy shared by all objects.
If Object 1 changes a static variable, Object 2 sees the change immediately.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In a Student class:
`studentName` is an instance variable (each student has a different name).
`totalEnrolledStudents` is static (counts the total students in the school).
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Remember: you can call static functions using `ClassName::functionName()`. You do not need to create an object with `new` first!
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Why can't static functions be `const`? Because `const` member functions promise not to modify the object pointed to by `this`. Since static functions have no `this` pointer, `const` makes no sense!
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
In C++17 and later, use `inline static int counter = 0;` inside the header to avoid duplicate symbol linker errors across translation units.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Static member functions have the standard C calling convention when not using member pointer syntax, allowing them to be passed as function pointer callbacks to C APIs (like `pthread_create` or `qsort`).
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A Connection Pool manager:
`DatabaseConnection` class has an `inline static int activeConnections = 0;`.
Every time a connection constructor runs, it increments `activeConnections`.
Every time a connection destructor runs, it decrements `activeConnections`.
`DatabaseConnection::getActiveCount()` reports the global pool usage anywhere in the application.
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class Counter {
public:
    static int objectCount; // Declaration of static variable

    Counter() {
        ++objectCount; // Increments shared count on each object creation
    }

    ~Counter() {
        --objectCount; // Decrements shared count on object destruction
    }

    static int getCount() { // Static member function
        return objectCount;
    }
};

// Definition of static member variable
int Counter::objectCount = 0;

int main() {
    std::cout << "Initial count: " << Counter::getCount() << "\n";

    Counter c1;
    Counter c2;
    Counter c3;

    std::cout << "Count after creating 3 objects: " << Counter::getCount() << "\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class User {
    int id;
    std::string username;
    static int nextId; // Generator for auto-incrementing IDs
public:
    explicit User(std::string name) : id(nextId++), username(std::move(name)) {}

    void display() const {
        std::cout << "User ID: " << id << " | Name: " << username << "\n";
    }

    static int getTotalUsersCreated() {
        return nextId - 1000;
    }
};

int User::nextId = 1000; // IDs start at 1000

int main() {
    User u1("Alice");
    User u2("Bob");
    User u3("Charlie");

    u1.display();
    u2.display();
    u3.display();

    std::cout << "Total users created: " << User::getTotalUsersCreated() << "\n";
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class MathUtility {
public:
    // Pure utility class with deleted default constructor
    MathUtility() = delete;

    static double square(double x) { return x * x; }
    static double cube(double x) { return x * x * x; }
    static constexpr double PI = 3.141592653589793;
};

int main() {
    // MathUtility m; // COMPILER ERROR: constructor is deleted!
    std::cout << "Square of 5: " << MathUtility::square(5) << "\n";
    std::cout << "Cube of 3:   " << MathUtility::cube(3) << "\n";
    std::cout << "Value of PI: " << MathUtility::PI << "\n";
    return 0;
}

The advanced example demonstrates a pure static utility class where object instantiation is disabled using `= delete`.

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_CPP_CONSTRUCTOR_TYPES_001
CATEGORY: Constructors and Destructors
SUBCATEGORY: Constructor Types and Move Semantics
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++, Java, C#, Python
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Deep Dive and Implementation
QUESTION: What are the different types of constructors in C++ (default, parameterized, copy, move), and how do copy and move constructors work?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
What are the types of constructors in C++?
Copy constructor vs move constructor in C++
Explain copy constructor with deep copy
Explain move constructor and rvalue references
Difference between shallow copy and deep copy
Move semantics in C++ constructors
Constructor overloading in C++
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
constructor types
default constructor
parameterized constructor
copy constructor
move constructor
rvalue reference
move semantics
deep copy vs shallow copy
rule of five
std::move
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
C++ provides four primary constructor types: Default constructors (take no arguments), Parameterized constructors (take initial values), Copy constructors (`T(const T&)`) which perform a deep copy of an existing object, and Move constructors (`T(T&&)`) introduced in C++11 which transfer resource ownership from a temporary rvalue without copying memory.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
Constructors initialize object state when memory is allocated:
1. Default Constructor: Initializes objects without arguments (e.g., `Point()`).
2. Parameterized Constructor: Takes arguments to set customized initial state (e.g., `Point(int x, int y)`).
3. Copy Constructor (`Point(const Point&)`): Duplicates an existing object. When classes manage heap memory, a copy constructor performs a **deep copy**, allocating a new heap buffer and copying data to avoid double-free errors.
4. Move Constructor (`Point(Point&&) noexcept`): Steals resources from a temporary object (rvalue). Instead of allocating new heap memory and copying bytes, it swaps pointers in $O(1)$ time and nulls out the source pointer, delivering massive performance gains.
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. The Four Types of Constructors:
- Default: `Class() = default;` or user-defined. Generated automatically if no constructors are declared.
- Parameterized: `Class(int a, string b);` Overloaded to accept varying configurations.
- Copy Constructor: `Class(const Class& other);` Invoked during pass-by-value, return-by-value (when RVO is disabled), or copy initialization (`Class a = b;`).
- Move Constructor: `Class(Class&& other) noexcept;` Invoked when initializing from an rvalue temporary (`Class a = std::move(b);`).

2. Shallow Copy vs Deep Copy:
- Shallow Copy: The default copy constructor copies members byte-by-byte. If the class holds a raw pointer `int* data`, both objects point to the EXACT same heap address! When both objects destruct, a fatal **double-free error** crashes the program.
- Deep Copy: Allocates new heap memory (`data = new int[*other.data];`) so both objects are completely independent.

3. Move Semantics & Rvalue References (`&&`):
- Move constructor signature: `Buffer(Buffer&& other) noexcept`
- Logic:
  `this->data = other.data;`   // Steal pointer
  `this->size = other.size;`
  `other.data = nullptr;`       // Nullify source so source destructor does nothing
  `other.size = 0;`
- Execution time: Instantaneous pointer copy ($O(1)$) instead of massive heap reallocation ($O(N)$).

4. The Rule of Five (C++11):
If a class manages resources and requires a custom Destructor, it almost always needs:
1. Destructor (`~Class()`)
2. Copy Constructor (`Class(const Class&)`)
3. Copy Assignment Operator (`Class& operator=(const Class&)`)
4. Move Constructor (`Class(Class&&) noexcept`)
5. Move Assignment Operator (`Class& operator=(Class&&) noexcept`)
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine building a toy castle!
- Default constructor: You open a box and it builds an empty green lawn.
- Parameterized constructor: You order a castle with 4 towers and a moat.
- Copy constructor: You carefully photocopy every single drawing and build an exact second castle with new plastic blocks.
- Move constructor: Someone gives you their old castle, and instead of rebuilding it, you just pick up the whole castle and place it in your bedroom, leaving their box empty! It's super fast!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Constructors set up your object:
- Default: no arguments.
- Parameterized: takes inputs.
- Copy: makes an exact twin copy.
- Move: steals resources from a temporary object so you don't have to duplicate memory.
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
If you have a dynamic array:
Copying means buying a new notebook and copying all 500 pages by hand.
Moving means taking the notebook directly and handing over the cover!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
In copy constructor: pass by reference `const T&`. If you accidentally passed by value `T`, it would call the copy constructor to copy the argument, causing infinite recursion!
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Always mark move constructors `noexcept`. Standard library containers like `std::vector` will only use your move constructor during resizing if it is guaranteed not to throw; otherwise, they fall back to slow copying!
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Copy-and-Swap idiom: You can implement copy and move assignment operators cleanly using a single pass-by-value assignment operator: `Class& operator=(Class temp) { swap(*this, temp); return *this; }`.
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
RVO (Return Value Optimization) and NRVO (Named Return Value Optimization) can elide both copy and move constructors entirely by constructing the return value directly in the caller's storage space.
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A dynamic string or audio buffer class (`DynamicBuffer`):
Managing an audio file containing 50 MB of raw audio samples.
When passing the audio buffer between processing threads, copying duplicates 50 MB of RAM ($O(N)$).
Moving transfers the 8-byte buffer pointer in less than 1 nanosecond ($O(1)$).
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

class Point {
    int x, y;
public:
    // 1. Default Constructor
    Point() : x(0), y(0) {
        std::cout << "Default constructor called\n";
    }

    // 2. Parameterized Constructor
    Point(int xVal, int yVal) : x(xVal), y(yVal) {
        std::cout << "Parameterized constructor called: (" << x << ", " << y << ")\n";
    }

    // 3. Copy Constructor
    Point(const Point& other) : x(other.x), y(other.y) {
        std::cout << "Copy constructor called: (" << x << ", " << y << ")\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    Point p1;             // Default
    Point p2(10, 20);     // Parameterized
    Point p3 = p2;        // Copy
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class DynamicArray {
    int* data;
    size_t size;
public:
    // Parameterized constructor
    explicit DynamicArray(size_t s) : size(s), data(new int[s]{}) {
        std::cout << "Allocated " << size << " integers on heap.\n";
    }

    // Deep Copy Constructor
    DynamicArray(const DynamicArray& other) : size(other.size), data(new int[other.size]) {
        std::cout << "Deep copy constructor: copying " << size << " integers.\n";
        for (size_t i = 0; i < size; ++i) {
            data[i] = other.data[i];
        }
    }

    // Destructor
    ~DynamicArray() {
        delete[] data;
        std::cout << "Freed memory.\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    DynamicArray arr1(5);
    DynamicArray arr2 = arr1; // Deep copy: safe from double-free!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <utility>

class Buffer {
    size_t size;
    int* data;
public:
    // Parameterized constructor
    Buffer(size_t s) : size(s), data(new int[s]{}) {
        std::cout << "Constructed Buffer of size " << size << "\n";
    }

    // Copy Constructor (Deep Copy)
    Buffer(const Buffer& other) : size(other.size), data(new int[other.size]) {
        std::cout << "Copy Constructor (deep copy " << size << " elements)\n";
        for (size_t i = 0; i < size; ++i) data[i] = other.data[i];
    }

    // Move Constructor (Resource Transfer - O(1))
    Buffer(Buffer&& other) noexcept : size(other.size), data(other.data) {
        std::cout << "Move Constructor (stole " << size << " elements, zero heap allocation!)\n";
        other.data = nullptr; // Null out source
        other.size = 0;
    }

    ~Buffer() {
        delete[] data;
    }
};

int main() {
    Buffer b1(1000000); // 1 million ints
    Buffer b2 = b1;      // Deep copy (expensive)
    Buffer b3 = std::move(b1); // Move constructor (instantaneous pointer steal!)
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example shows default, parameterized, and copy constructors for a 2D Point. The medium example shows how a deep copy constructor prevents double-free errors when managing raw heap pointers. The advanced example compares deep copying with C++11 move semantics, proving that move constructor transfers ownership in $O(1)$ time without allocating new heap memory.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
Default constructor called
Parameterized constructor called: (10, 20)
Copy constructor called: (10, 20)
[medium]
Allocated 5 integers on heap.
Deep copy constructor: copying 5 integers.
Freed memory.
Freed memory.
[advanced]
Constructed Buffer of size 1000000
Copy Constructor (deep copy 1000000 elements)
Move Constructor (stole 1000000 elements, zero heap allocation!)
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT
COMMON_MISTAKES<<
Writing a copy constructor taking `T` by value instead of `const T&`, causing compile-time infinite recursion.
Performing a shallow copy on classes managing raw pointer resources, causing double-free crashes upon destruction.
Forgetting to nullify the source pointer in the move constructor, causing the source destructor to delete the stolen resource.
Forgetting `noexcept` on move constructors.
>>COMMON_MISTAKES
RELATED_TOPICS<<

Memory Management
RAII
Rule of Five
>>RELATED_TOPICS
FOLLOW_UP_QUESTIONS<<

=====RECORD_BEGIN=====
RECORD_ID: OOP_CPP_VIRTUAL_DESTRUCTOR_001
CATEGORY: Constructors and Destructors
SUBCATEGORY: Virtual Destructors and Cleanup
PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGE: C++
SUPPORTED_PROGRAMMING_LANGUAGES: C++
HUMAN_LANGUAGE: English
DIFFICULTY: Advanced
QUESTION_TYPE: Deep Dive and Memory Safety
QUESTION: Why do we use virtual destructors in C++ base classes, and what happens if a base class destructor is not virtual?
ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS<<
Why virtual destructor in C++?
When should a destructor be virtual?
What happens if base class destructor is not virtual?
Virtual destructor explanation and example
Virtual destructor and memory leaks
Undefined behavior deleting derived class through base pointer
>>ALTERNATE_QUESTIONS
SEARCH_TERMS<<
virtual destructor
virtual ~Base()
polymorphic deletion
memory leak
undefined behavior
base class pointer
destructor call order
virtual table destructor
>>SEARCH_TERMS
ANSWER_SHORT<<
A base class destructor MUST be declared `virtual` whenever the class contains any virtual functions and objects of derived classes will be deleted through a pointer to the base class (`Base* p = new Derived(); delete p;`). If the destructor is not virtual, `delete p` invokes only the base class destructor, leaving the derived class's destructor uncalled, causing severe resource leaks and undefined behavior.
>>ANSWER_SHORT
ANSWER_MEDIUM<<
In C++, deleting an object through a pointer to its base class (`Base* p = new Derived(); delete p;`) requires dynamic dispatch to resolve the correct destructor. If `Base` does not declare its destructor as `virtual`, the compiler performs static binding based on the pointer type (`Base*`). Consequently, only `~Base()` executes, and `~Derived()` is completely bypassed. Any dynamic memory, open file handles, database locks, or network sockets allocated in `Derived` will never be cleaned up, causing memory leaks and undefined behavior according to the C++ standard. Adding `virtual ~Base() = default;` ensures that destructors execute in proper bottom-up order (`~Derived()` then `~Base()`).
>>ANSWER_MEDIUM
ANSWER_DETAILED<<
1. The C++ Standard Rule (ISO/IEC 14882 [expr.delete]):
"If the static type of the object to be deleted is different from its dynamic type, the static type shall be a base class of the dynamic type of the object to be deleted and the static type shall have a virtual destructor or the behavior is undefined."
Notice: It is not merely a memory leak; it is formally **Undefined Behavior** (UB)!

2. Execution Flow With vs Without Virtual Destructor:
Scenario: `Base* p = new Derived(); delete p;`
- Without `virtual ~Base()`:
  Only `Base::~Base()` is called.
  `Derived::~Derived()` is NEVER called.
- With `virtual ~Base()`:
  Step 1: Compiler queries `p->vptr->vtable` to locate the derived destructor.
  Step 2: `Derived::~Derived()` executes and cleans up derived resources.
  Step 3: `Base::~Base()` executes automatically and cleans up base resources.

3. When is a Virtual Destructor NOT Needed?
If a class is NOT intended to be a base class, or if it will never be deleted polymorphically via a base pointer, making the destructor virtual adds an unnecessary 8-byte `vptr` and vtable overhead.
Guideline: "A base class destructor should be either public and virtual, or protected and non-virtual" (Sutter & Alexandrescu).
- Public and Virtual: If client code is expected to delete derived objects through `Base*`.
- Protected and Non-Virtual: If client code should NEVER delete through `Base*` directly, preventing polymorphic deletion mistakes at compile time.
>>ANSWER_DETAILED
LEVEL_1_CHILD<<
Imagine you rent a two-story dollhouse! The ground floor is Base, and the top floor is Derived. When you decide to pack up and destroy the dollhouse, if your cleaning robot only knows about the ground floor, it only cleans up the bottom floor and leaves all the toys and trash scattered on the top floor forever! Making the cleaner 'virtual' teaches the robot to look up and clean the top floor first, then clean the bottom floor!
>>LEVEL_1_CHILD
LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER<<
Rule: Whenever a class has `virtual` functions, ALWAYS make the destructor `virtual`:
`virtual ~Base() = default;`
If you forget this, deleting derived objects through a base pointer will leak memory!
>>LEVEL_2_ABSOLUTE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT<<
In a school project: Derived class opens a database file. If the base destructor is not virtual, the database file stays locked open in memory even after the object is deleted!
>>LEVEL_3_SCHOOL_STUDENT
LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER<<
Syntax:
```cpp
class Base {
public:
    virtual ~Base() = default; // C++11 default virtual destructor
};
```
>>LEVEL_4_COLLEGE_BEGINNER
LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE<<
Virtual destructors occupy a slot in the class's vtable just like any other virtual function. In fact, most compilers generate two destructor entries in the vtable: the complete object destructor and the deleting destructor.
>>LEVEL_5_INTERMEDIATE
LEVEL_6_ADVANCED<<
Protected non-virtual destructor alternative:
```cpp
class Base {
protected:
    ~Base() = default; // Prevents delete basePtr; at compile time!
};
```
>>LEVEL_6_ADVANCED
LEVEL_7_EXPERT<<
Pure Virtual Destructors: A class can be made abstract without declaring any other pure virtual functions by declaring its destructor pure virtual:
```cpp
class AbstractBase {
public:
    virtual ~AbstractBase() = 0;
};
inline AbstractBase::~AbstractBase() {} // Must have a body!
```
>>LEVEL_7_EXPERT
EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD<<
A Plugin Architecture:
`Plugin` base class in an audio workstation (DAW).
Third-party plugins derive from `Plugin` (e.g. `SynthesizerPlugin : public Plugin`).
`SynthesizerPlugin` allocates 100 MB of audio sample waveforms on the heap.
When the user deletes the track, the DAW executes `delete pluginPtr;`.
With a virtual destructor, `~SynthesizerPlugin()` executes and frees the 100 MB.
Without a virtual destructor, the 100 MB leaks completely every time a track is closed!
>>EXAMPLE_REAL_WORLD
EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

class BaseBad {
public:
    ~BaseBad() { std::cout << "~BaseBad() executed\n"; } // NOT virtual!
};

class DerivedBad : public BaseBad {
    int* data;
public:
    DerivedBad() : data(new int[100]) { std::cout << "DerivedBad allocated 100 ints\n"; }
    ~DerivedBad() {
        delete[] data;
        std::cout << "~DerivedBad() executed (memory freed)\n";
    }
};

class BaseGood {
public:
    virtual ~BaseGood() { std::cout << "~BaseGood() executed\n"; } // Virtual!
};

class DerivedGood : public BaseGood {
    int* data;
public:
    DerivedGood() : data(new int[100]) { std::cout << "DerivedGood allocated 100 ints\n"; }
    ~DerivedGood() override {
        delete[] data;
        std::cout << "~DerivedGood() executed (memory freed)\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    std::cout << "--- 1. BAD: Non-virtual destructor (Leaking Derived) ---\n";
    BaseBad* b1 = new DerivedBad();
    delete b1; // NOTICE: ~DerivedBad() NEVER RUNS! Memory leaked!

    std::cout << "\n--- 2. GOOD: Virtual destructor (Clean Destruction) ---\n";
    BaseGood* b2 = new DerivedGood();
    delete b2; // Both ~DerivedGood() and ~BaseGood() execute properly!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_SIMPLE_CODE
EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE<<
#include <iostream>
#include <memory>
#include <string>

class FileStreamer {
public:
    virtual void stream() const { std::cout << "Streaming bytes...\n"; }
    virtual ~FileStreamer() {
        std::cout << "FileStreamer base closed.\n";
    }
};

class EncryptedFileStreamer : public FileStreamer {
    std::string encryptionKey;
public:
    explicit EncryptedFileStreamer(std::string key) : encryptionKey(std::move(key)) {
        std::cout << "EncryptedFileStreamer initialized with key: " << encryptionKey << "\n";
    }

    ~EncryptedFileStreamer() override {
        std::cout << "EncryptedFileStreamer wiped key from RAM and flushed secure buffer.\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    // Smart pointers with polymorphic base classes
    std::unique_ptr<FileStreamer> streamer = 
        std::make_unique<EncryptedFileStreamer>("AES-256-SECRET-KEY");
    streamer->stream();
    // Unique pointer deletes polymorphically at end of scope!
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_MEDIUM_CODE
EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE<<
#include <iostream>

// Pure Virtual Destructor to make a class abstract
class AbstractInterface {
public:
    virtual void action() = 0;
    virtual ~AbstractInterface() = 0; // Pure virtual destructor
};

// Pure virtual destructor MUST have a definition outside the class!
AbstractInterface::~AbstractInterface() {
    std::cout << "AbstractInterface destructor body executed.\n";
}

class ConcreteImplementation : public AbstractInterface {
public:
    void action() override {
        std::cout << "Concrete action executed.\n";
    }
    ~ConcreteImplementation() override {
        std::cout << "ConcreteImplementation destructor executed.\n";
    }
};

int main() {
    AbstractInterface* ptr = new ConcreteImplementation();
    ptr->action();
    delete ptr; // Polymorphically calls Concrete then Abstract destructor
    return 0;
}
>>EXAMPLE_ADVANCED_CODE
CODE_EXPLANATION<<
The simple example contrasts deleting a derived object through a non-virtual base pointer (where ~DerivedBad() never runs) vs a virtual base pointer (where ~DerivedGood() runs and frees memory). The medium example shows polymorphic destruction using `std::unique_ptr`. The advanced example demonstrates implementing an abstract class via a pure virtual destructor with an out-of-class definition.
>>CODE_EXPLANATION
EXPECTED_OUTPUT<<
[simple]
--- 1. BAD: Non-virtual destructor (Leaking Derived) ---
DerivedBad allocated 100 ints
~BaseBad() executed

--- 2. GOOD: Virtual destructor (Clean Destruction) ---
DerivedGood allocated 100 ints
~DerivedGood() executed (memory freed)
~BaseGood() executed
[medium]
EncryptedFileStreamer initialized with key: AES-256-SECRET-KEY
Streaming bytes...
EncryptedFileStreamer wiped key from RAM and flushed secure buffer.
FileStreamer base closed.
[advanced]
Concrete action executed.
ConcreteImplementation destructor executed.
AbstractInterface destructor body executed.
>>EXPECTED_OUTPUT
COMMON_MISTAKES<<
Failing to declare virtual destructors in polymorphic base classes, causing undefined behavior and resource leaks.
Declaring all destructors virtual needlessly in classes never meant for inheritance, adding 8 bytes of vptr overhead.
Declaring a pure virtual destructor without providing a function body, causing linker errors.
